IV. Time for objecting to witness's qualification, p. 1384. 

I. Generally. Many common law regulations which prohibited persons from taking the witness stand, have been abolished. The usual rule now is that any normal adult person who is capable of understanding an oath may be a witness. The 

1"Any person of sufficient understanding, although a party, may testify in any proceeding, civil or criminal, in court or before a person who has authority to receive evidence" except in certain instances enumerated. 

G. L. Ch. 233, Sec. 20. 

2 As to minors, see note 31. 

8 See note 1 above. Com. v. Tatisos, 238 Mass. 322. 1373

effect of conviction of crime, of disbelief in God," of direct interest in the outcome of the case, is no longer to disqualify. 

There are controversies which involve the qualifications of witnesses, however, that fairly frequently arise. 

II. Who may be witnesses. The question who may be a witness is now without complication. Under the present law any person with sufficient understanding to realize the obligation of an oath may testify. This rule as applied to certain officers and classes of persons should be more minutely considered. A. Judge as witness. A judge, of course, may be a witness." 

If he testifies as to matters which he has observed as an ordinary citizen, apparently he should take the stand as an ordinary witness. If he testifies as to matters which pertain to his official action as a judge in a court other than that in which the case is now on trial, he probably testifies from the witness stand." 

If he testifies as to matters which have come under his official action in the case now on trial, and in the court wherein the case is now tried, he may testify from the bench.10 This does not

mean that the judge who is actually sitting as the trial court should testify in the case which he is hearing. He should not do so.11 Neither does it mean that a judge is bound to respond on summons and testify as to his official acts or records.12 

B. Jurors as witnesses. A juror or a grand juror may be a witness.18 Such practice, however, is to be discouraged.14 A case wherein a member of a panel must be called as a witness should be transferred to another session. This does not mean that a juror may testify to matters that are privileged under the rule which forbids a jury or a juror to disclose what happened in the jury room.15

C. Attorneys as witnesses. An attorney at law, though actually engaged in the trial 16 of the case, or associated 17 with the attorney who is actually trying, may be a witness. However, such proceeding is not to be encouraged.18 The Rules of the 

Superior Court of Massachusetts provide that "No attorney shall be permitted to take part in the conduct of a case in which he is a witness for his client except by special leave of the court." 19 In the District Courts the same rule exists.20 If an attorney tries a case in which he is a party it seems to be presumed that as of right he may testify." 21 If a party intends to call his adversary's attorney as a witness, he should notify his adversary in advance, giving him an opportunity to procure other counsel.2 22 D. If a person is not normal in appearance or behavior, perhaps there is a duty on the trial court, even without objection as to his qualifications, to determine whether he is of sufficient capacity to testify.24 If a witness is a patient in an institution for treatment of insanity the usual presumption of capacity to testify seems to be overcome; 25 but ordinarily the burden of showing incapacity, says high authority, rests on the person who objects to the witness as totally incapable.26 Discharge from an insane asylum may be prima facie evidence of recovery of mental faculties and of ability to testify.2 27 Minors of tender years are not capable to testify; 28 yet at what age the line is to be drawn is not determined.29 Much is left to the discretion of the trial court.80 Probably a minor under seven years of age is prima facie assumed incapable to observe, to remember, to understand an oath, and to testify. 81

​2 There may be no presumption as to a minor between the ages of seven and fourteen.32 A minor over fourteen will be prima facie presumed to be competent.38 The court may examine a minor to determine his qualification, or may allow counsel to conduct the examination.34 In some instances, perhaps, it is his duty to do so without request.35 On such examination, even though the court finds that the minor does not understand the nature of an oath, yet he may cause the minor to be instructed in that regard, and may postpone his final finding until after that instruction is completed. If he then finds that the minor is capable, and understands the nature of an oath, he may permit him to testify.36 The court's findings and rulings will not be readily disturbed. There are, however, limits to his power to declare a witness qualified.88 The practice on the continent of Europe which permits young children to testify without oath, though approved by some authority as feasible, is not accepted in the United States.39 40 87 41 Persons who are mentally deranged are not always incapable of testifying. The tests in such case may include ability to observe, ability to remember, ability to state clearly and accurately 42 the matter observed and remembered, and ability to comprehend and to respect the obligations of an oath. ance, behavior, method of speech, or by testimony.45 When such disqualification seems probable, the trial court should cause the witness's ability to be investigated.46 If he does not, however, and no objection to the witness has been raised, the adversary probably cannot insist that he is injured. To take advantage of a witness's incapacity, an objection to his qualifications should be recorded, and exceptions to an adverse ruling taken.48 An inmate of an insane asylum or one adjudged insane, prima facie is presumed incapable to testify.49 The witness's discharge from an insane asylum, probably creates a presumption of his present sanity.50 In considering a motion for a new trial, a report of the department of mental diseases has been considered as evidence in determining the status of a defendant.51 Insanity of a proposed witness, prior to his offer as a witness, seems immaterial except as it may be evidence in and of itself of a present insanity, or of prior insanity which would affect powers at the time of the event of which he is to testify, to observe and remember.52 If a witness is sane at the time of trial he can make clear the indefiniteness and inaccuracy of his recollection; hence it seems that this should be the time which should determine the test of his capacity to be a witness.53 

Feeble minded persons are to be treated in this respect much like insane persons. The court on examination usually determines the person's ability to testify and his finding will not be readily disturbed. A recent case, however, should be noted. know the difference between right and wrong, had never heard of a Supreme Being, did not know what the word God meant, had never heard of the hereafter, and did not know what the obligation of an oath was, and what would happen if she did not tell the truth except that she would be in trouble. She testified that she knew what an oath was because she could see it in court. She did not realize what the defendant was doing at the time of the intercourse and did not know for a considerable time of her pregnancy. Experts testified that she was not mentally deranged, could read and write a simple letter, and make simple arithmetical computations, and that she was definitely defective in judgment and comprehension. At the time of the trial she was an inmate of the State School for the Feeble Minded. She should not have been permitted to testify and to establish such accusations on her unsupported testimony." E. Drunkenness as affecting qualifications. A person under the influence of liquor, even while intoxicated, has been permitted to testify.55 In considering questions which concern drunkenness, principles similar to those which apply when the qualification depends on insanity, arise. The question of a witness's qualification to testify may depend on his state of intoxication and power of observation at the time of the act in issue,56 or on his ability to remember," or on his ability to understand and to think clearly at the time of his testifying.58 

Such has been suggested.59 Much lies within the discretion of the trial court.60 However the limit of the judge's discretion should be reached if the witness at the time of testifying, is

F. Parties. Parties and persons in interest are no longer excluded from the right to testify.64 A party in a civil action may call his adversary and cross examine him.65 If he does so he is not absolutely bound by his testimony.66

I. Infamy, accomplices, accessories, "spotters." Lack of corroborating testimony, infamy, commission of crime, participation by an accomplice or accessory in the commission of an offence for which a defendant is on trial, do not render a witness incompetent." They may affect his credibility." When accessories or persons who have induced the defendant to commit the crime in issue appear as witnesses, they are competent; but "the general rule of practice is to caution a jury not to convict where the evidence consists of uncorroborated testimony of an accomplice. The court however in its discretion may refuse such instruction." 78 The same general principle applies to "spotters." In prosecutions for fraudulently and deceitfully enticing certain females under General Laws, Chapter 272, Sec tions 2 to 6, a person shall not be convicted on the evidence of one witness only unless his testimony is corroborated in a material particular.75

J. General rule: Matters not to be confused. The many disqualifications which by the common law barred various persons from the witness stand no longer apply. "Any person of sufficient understanding" now may be a witness.76 This must not be confused with the principle which limits a witness in the matters to which he may testify. A man may be eligible as a witness and yet may not be qualified as an expert." Until his qualification as an expert has been shown, he cannot testify as an expert as to opinion. So the principle must not be confused with matters which affect credibility. Many matters which at common law precluded a person as a witness, may now be shown to affect his credibility and to impeach him.78

K. Experts and opinion. The qualifications which entitle a person who appears as a proposed witness, to testify as to facts observed by him, are to be distinguished from the qualifications which permit one to testify as an expert as to matters of opinion. In the former there is a presumption that a person is of See

III. Province of court and jury in determining qualifications of witness-A. Generally. The court, and not the jury, unless the "human practice" which applies in certain criminal proceedings 82 is involved, determines whether a proposed witness is disqualified to testify.88 Here arise several questions-first is the person at the time when he is offered as a witness, barred by mental incapacity from testifying,84 second was he at the time of the act of which he is to testify of sufficient capacity to observe,85 third has some intervening cause so affected his memory or reason that he cannot distinguish between the truth and falsity of events which his mind suggests as occurrences.86 

What is the province of the judge in the first case, is clear. If the person is not of sufficient mental capacity to understand and respect the obligation of an oath, he is to be excluded as a witness.87 The answer to the second question is not so easy; on this there is a conflict of authority.88 Here the inquiry does not affect the person's qualification as a witness, but only his qualification as to testifying to certain events. An illustration of this principle would arise were a child called as a witness. 

The judge, within his discretion, may determine that he is capable to testify; but the questions put to the witness may call for facts which occurred when the child was too young to ob-

Credibility is usually for the jury, and if a person sworn to testify to the truth, on his oath declares that he recalls an event as a positive occurrence, his credibility usually is for the jury. Distinction in civil and criminal cases. In civil cases, as explaned heretofore, the trial judge and not the jury passes on preliminary questions which involve the admissibility of evidence. The jury cannot override him. In criminal cases, the "human procedure" referred to above, permits the jury, if the court allows a witness to testify and admits certain evidence, to reconsider the propriety of the court's findings of fact, and requires the jury to dismiss from their consideration the evidence which the trial judge has admitted, if it determines that he was in error in his findings on preliminary facts.95

C. Burden of proof. The burden of showing a witness's qualifications should be on the proponent. When the question is whether one offered as an expert is qualified, the burden of proof always rests on him.96 When mental capacity is involved, where the burden of proving capability lies is not so clear. Authority places the burden of showing incapacity on the opponent.97 Perhaps the rule is that mental capacity to testify as an ordinary witness is presumed, until the contrary ap- 89 The court may exclude evidence of statements made in a person's presence, the truth of which the person did not deny, if the circumstances were such that he would not be expected to deny the statement. Two matters are to be repeated. If a proposed witness is an inmate of an insane asylum, or under an unmodified decree of a court has been declared insane, the presumption of insanity does not prevail. 100 If, however, the witness has been discharged from the asylum as cured,¹º¹ or if the decree of insanity has been modified and the person declared sane, the presumption of sanity renews.

IV. Time for objecting to witness's qualification. On the general principle of procedure,102 it appears that an objection to a witness as incapable should be made before he is sworn, or as soon as his incapacity becomes evident by his appearance, speech, or behaviour. If a preliminary qualification is required, as in the case of an alleged expert, the objection should be made as soon as any question which the witness has not been shown capable of answering, is asked.108

I. Introductory: Witness's general status. Credibility is for the jury or other fact-determining body.¹ To enable such body to determine what witness is telling the truth, the jury should have knowledge of the kind of person that is before it 

1 See Part I, Chapter 2.

as a witness. made.

Hence general inquiry along this line may be For this purpose a person's method and habits of life properly may be presented. So an inquiry as to the size of a witness's family," his habits, and idiosyncrasies is allowable. 

The extent of such inquiry is within the court's discretion." 

In determining the credibility of a witness two questions arise, How far can evidence be presented to impeach the witness; how far can evidence be presented to corroborate him. 

About these two questions formulate the rules of evidence which touch credibility.

impeached. The answer to this question depends partly on common law rules and partly on statute, Usually, but not always, a party is bound by his own testimony; a man should not be permitted to impeach himself. At common law if one called a witness, he could not impeach him. At common law a party could not be a witness, hence, an adversary could not be called to be cross examined; no interrogatories to the adverse party were permitted, and one could not impeach one's own witness. 

B. Impeaching opponent's witness. From the earliest days the opponent's witnesses could be impeached. This was and is, one of the chief reasons for full and complete cross examination. The method of impeachment, and the subject which may be introduced to impeach such witness, are considered later. 

It is to be noted that in impeaching a witness on purely collateral matters much is left to the discretion of the court, and that usually the court will not permit the introduction of testimony of witnesses other than the witness who is to be impeached, to present evidence which contradicts the witness on purely collateral matters." If the immaterial evidence which the adversary desires to impeach is brought out for the first time on cross examination, the court probably cannot allow a third person to be called by the cross-examining party solely to refute this immaterial matter.18

C. Impeaching own witness. Though at common law one could not impeach one's own witness, by a statute such impeachment is now allowed.18 Before a party seeks to contradict his own witness by evidence of a prior statement contradictory of his present testimony, he must mention to the witness the circumstances of the inconsistent statement with sufficient particularity 14 to designate the occasion when the inconsistent statement was made, must ask the witness if he made the inconsistent statement,15 and if the witness admits that he did make such inconsistent statement, must allow him to explain it.16 "A party who produces a witness shall not impeach his credit by evidence of bad character but may contradict him by other evidence," 17 and subject to the procedure explained above, may prove that he has made at other times statements inconsistent with his present testimony.18 The wording of the statute referred to above 19 leaves open two matters. The statute provides that a party may contradict his own witness "by other evidence," but requires that if the impeaching evidence consists of an "inconsistent statement” made by the witness, the witness's attention shall be called to the circumstances which surround its utterance and he shall be allowed to explain. If the impeachment is by the witness's inconsistent acts, or by his silence 20 in the face of accusation or statement inconsistent with his present testimony by others in the presence of the witness, may this impeaching evidence be admitted without calling the witness's attention to the surrounding circumstances? As such evidence is not a "statement" made by the witness probably it may be admitted without the preliminary questions required by the statute. When a witness calls the adversary to cross examine him may he, without following the procedure set by this statute, contradict him or impeach him? Probably yes. The enactment of the statute which allows the impeachment of one's own witnesses occurred prior 21 to the act which allows a party

In interpreting the statute now under consideration several matters are to be noted. The "inconsistent statement" must be inconsistent with evidence material to the issue,-inconsistent immaterial statements probably are not to be admitted.28 

A mere statement by a witness that he does not remember his alleged prior statement, is not to be contradicted by proof that he made the alleged statement at a previous time. The statute applies to criminal as well as civil cases.25 It is the inconsistency of the statements and not the surprise to the proponent that determines the proponent's right to show the inconsistency.28 Hence, a party may call a witness and ask him under oath to testify to matters which he believes the witness, if he tells the truth, will answer favorably to that party.27 On his failure to do so later he may accuse him of perjury and on proper precedure at the present trial may introduce evidence of his inconsistent statement. Such inconsistent statement, however, made out of court and not under oath is not evidence of the truth of the facts set forth in the statement.28 An unfair advantage may be taken by this principle; 29 against such unfairness the trial court should carefully guard by proper instructions.30 "This statute, unless strictly conformed to, ap-

In an accident suit the plaintiff after the close of the defendant's case called a claim agent of the defendant and asked him if he had had a certain conversation with the plaintiff wherein he inferred that others, including a priest, had stated that the plaintiff at the time of the accident had been drinking. D. Impeaching adversary's testimony. Of course an ad versary called as a witness may be impeached by any of the usual methods of impeachment.32 It is to be noted that usually dence admitted that the witness had said in the defendant's presence, after his arrest, that the defendant was the man who assaulted him. The defendant apparently remained silent. Court instructed jury that this evidence was merely to contradict the witness and not evidence of guilt. Court holds defendant's rights fully protected. Commonwealth v. McIntosh, 259 Mass. 389.

in the contradiction of evidence which is purely collateral, much is left to the discretion of the court.88 In cross examination the court may limit the inquiry into collateral matters, and usually will not permit the opponent to call a witness to contradict an adversary or his witnesses on purely collateral matters.84 Immaterial matters brought out for the first time in cross examination, in spite of dictum to the contrary, probably cannot be refuted by witnesses other than the witness who gave the testimony or witnesses called by the party who called that witness; if the matter is not brought out for the first time in cross examination, it might be thus rebutted.85 

E. Impeaching adversary's answers to interrogatories. 

Usually a party who puts in evidence his opponent's answers to interrogatories is bound by those answers if they are not contradicted. There seems, however, no reason why these answers cannot be contradicted by the party who presents them. 

F. Impeaching one not a witness at present trial. Auditor's reports,―agreements as to what absent witness would testify to. Though a party has called a witness at a former hearing of the same case, if that witness is not called at the present hearing, he cannot be impeached. Hence, even when a witness has testified before an auditor, though the auditor's report at the present trial is put in evidence, yet the witness not called to the stand in the present trial, cannot by new evidence be impeached.40 If a proponent's witness is absent and the parties to avoid continuance agree as to what he would testify to, the opponent though he may not dispute that the witness if present would so testify, yet may impeach his agreed evidence and question its truth.41 v. III. By what evidence may witness be impeached-A. Introductory. The court will not consider every matter that might tend to show a witness's evidence untrue. A witness is not required to defend every act of his life against a cross examination, or to be tried on mere opinion as to his character or possible dishonesty.45 Certain lines for proper impeachment are established. These are now considered. 48 B. Evidence on cross examination. The most common way of impeaching a witness is by his cross examination.48 His contradiction of his direct examination may affect the jury's opinion of his credibility.47 Though he is shown to be wilfully lying, however, the jury may believe a part of his testimony. "Falsus in uno falsus in omnibus" is not a rule of law in Massachusetts. The jury, or other fact-trying body,50 may believe a witness in part and disbelieve him in part.51 A cross examination may be directed to bring out any matter admissible as shown below, to affect credibility. It is to be remembered that in determining the extent of cross examination much is left to the trial court's discretion.52

C. Prior inconsistent statement-1. Adversary's witness. 

A statement inconsistent 58 with a witness's present testimony, party admitting, to avoid continuance, that absent witness will testify as stated in affidavit, cannot contradict statement that witness would so testify, but may show that such testimony is contrary to facts and introduce evidence of witness's statements after as well as before affidavits was filed to disprove or discredit statements in affidavit. If the attempt is made then to impeach an adversary's witness no preliminary inquiry of the witness as to his making the inconsistent statement is necessary.58 He need be given no chance prior to the introduction of the inconsistent statement to explain. It is to be noted that a witness's failure to remember a fact is not evidence that the fact exists, and hence that the witness's statement in which he discloses the fact, is not admissible to impeach his testimony.59 

2. Impeaching own witness-a. Generally. The party who produces a witness shall not impeach his credit by evidence of bad character, but may contradict him by other evidence, and may also prove that he has made at other times statements inconsistent with his present testimony; but before proof of such inconsistent statements is given, the circumstances thereof sufficient to designate the particular occasion shall be mentioned to the witness, and he shall be asked if he has made such statements, and, if so, shall be allowed to explain them.61 When this has been done such evidence to impeach the witness is admissible. b. Circumstances "sufficient to designate the particular occasion": Exactness. The statute requires that before the witness shall be impeached the circumstance of his inconsistent statement sufficient to designate the particular occasion shall be called to his attention.65 The party examining the witness in this preliminary matter should be exact, should refer to the particular statement, and should not enlarge on the exact inconargue that it was inconsistent with his testimony, and had a legitimate tendency to impeach the credibility of the witness. It was therefore material. (Citing Robinson v. O. C. St. Ry. Co., 189 Mass. 594, and cases cited.)

It is "his duty to direct the attention of the witness to the specific evidence which he desires to contradict, and to question him respecting only such previous statements as were inconsistent with the evidence and to comply in other respects with the terms of the statute in making the inquiry." 66 c. Effect of witness's admission that he made the statement. 

The witness should be asked whether he made the statement; if he says that he did, no evidence of other witnesses is to be presented to show what the statement was.67 If he admits that he made the statement, he must be allowed to explain it and the inconsistency.68 

d. Effect of witness's denial that he made the statement. If 

If the witness denies that he made the statement, and the evidence which is inconsistent with the statement is material,69 other witnesses may be called to testify that the witness made the inconsistent statement and as to what it was.70 The facts in this statement are not thus put in evidence. The inconsistent statement goes solely to credibility of the witness, and has no probative force." If the inconsistent statement contradicts immaterial evidence, it probably must be excluded.72

It is to be noted that if the witness's attention has not been called to the prior inconsistent statement and the circumstances which attended it with exactness, evidence of another called to state what the alleged inconsistent statement was, is not admissible. The impeaching witness can testify only to an inconsistent statement which with particularity and exactness has been called to the attention of the witness to be impeached.78 

It is to be noted also that the statutory requirement that the witness's attention be called to the statement is not a substitute for the doctrine of refreshing recollection."

The defendant offered to show by a police officer that the latter was told by Swan (who testified for the plaintiff) that at the time of the accident they were "swapping wheels and stalled, run on the track and stalled the engine"; this evidence was excluded and the defendant excepted. The record does not disclose that the evidence contained in the offer of proof had any tendency to contradict anything testified to by Swan; he testified that he did not remember what he said to the officer as to how it happened. This exception must be overruled. Labrecque v. Donham, 236 Mass. 10 at 13. "The issue of the defendant's identity was material and when confronted with the defendant, the witness having denied that he was the doctor who telephoned or visited the house, the Commonwealth, after calling her attention thereto, was properly permitted to show prior contradictory or inconsistent statements made to police officers in which she stated that the defendant was the person with whom she dealt and that at the first trial she testified that the doctor who called at the house 'looked like the defendant.' The evidence of a police officer also tending to identify her as the woman seen talking in the street with the defendant after the operation had been performed was admissible. He had testified that he did not remember whether he had made any report about the car, or whether he had reported that the car was no good.

remember," a statement made by him immediately before he took the stand in which he described the event which he now claims that he does not remember, certainly seems inconsistent with his present testimony. Adversary's witness. The D. Prior inconsistent silence-1. 

prior silence of an opponent's witness when he should have disstated that he had made such a report was incompetent. All this testimony was highly prejudicial to the defendant.",

Corsick v. Boston Elevated, 218 

Mass. 144. Defendant seeks to impeach his own witness. Provotkin, the witness sought to be impeached, examined by the defendant, denied that he had any conversation with either of the plaintiffs regarding the case; stated that he did not recall having any conversation with Bornstein, the impeaching witness, that he was "almost" sure that he did not "overhear Mrs.

Bloustein (the plaintiff) say anything about this case to anybody," that he 'couldn't tell" whether he heard her say anything about how this case happened; that he did not hear her say anything, about how she had the miscarriage, and that he did not recall that at any time he had told Bornstein a different story. The witness Bornstein, against the exception of the plaintiffs was permitted to testify that Provotkin did have a conversation with the witness at the house about the case. He was then, against the exception of the plaintiffs, permitted to testify that he (Provotkin) had told him (the witness) that he had overheard the plaintiff Mrs. Bloustein say to one Mrs. Berman, "that she had a fight with a little boy who kicked her and from that she had a miscarriage.'' 

The testimony of what Provotkin told 

Bornstein he had overheard Mrs. Bloustein say to Mrs. Berman was not inconsistent with the testimony of Provotkin that he was unable to recall that he had stated to Bornstein a different story from that he stated in court; that he did not remember saying anything different to Bornstein; that he did not hear her (Mrs.

Bloustein) say anything about how the case happened or about how she had the miscarriage. In a word, the testimony of witness was colorless; he had given no testimony in favor of the plaintiff against the contention of the defendant. The rights of the defendant were fully protected by the admission of the testimony of the impeaching witness that Provotkin had talked to him about the case and had told him that he had overheard a conversation between Mrs. Bloustein and 

Mrs. Berman regarding the miscarriage.

The admission of the conversation itself was not in contradiction of any testimony of the witness Provotkin, and was highly prejudicial to the plaintiffs. The case cannot be distinguished from and is governed by, Corsick v. 

Boston Elevated Ry., 218 Mass. 144; 

Bloustein v. Shindler, 235 Mass. 441.

2. One's own witness. The fair interpretation of the statute which allows a party to impeach his own witness probably allows the impeachment by showing the witness's unwarranted silence.82 As such silence is not a "statement" it may be that the proponent need not call the witness's attention to the inconsistency and give him a chance to explain before he introduces the impeaching evidence.88

2. Of adversary's lay witness. When a person expresses an opinion such as that one is at fault, this is not a statement of fact but of opinion.86 He could not have testified to this. Assume that a witness has made such statement, and that he now testifies to facts which show that the party accused was not at fault, may he be impeached by his former statement? If the opinion was clearly inconsistent with his testimony, yes.87 "It is enough if an opinion has been expressed by a witness which is so incompatible with the facts which he has testified to as a witness that an honest mind knowing the facts would not be likely to entertain the opinion." 88

Mass. 1. Commonwealth v. Baxter, June 8, 1929, A. S. 1367, Mass. -; 

166 N. E. 742. Evidence affecting the credibility of the defendant as a witness was admissible. But the examination of the witnesses called by the plaintiff in impeachment was restricted to their knowledge of the defendant's general reputation in the community for truth and veracity, and evidence of particular acts showing him to be untruthful were inadmissible. The reasons generally given are, that proof of separate instances of falsehood may have existed without impairing his general reputation for truthfulness; or that the impeached witness is not required to be prepared to meet particular acts of which he has had no notice although he is presumed to be capable of supporting his general reputation; or that the attention of jurors will be distracted from the real issue, to be tried by the introduction of collateral issues, which also would tend to prolong the trial unduly. (Citing, Eastman v. Boston Elevated, 200 Mass. 412, 413, and cases cited; Commonwealth v. 

Lawlor, 12 Allen 585; Commonwealth v. Rogers, 136 Mass. 158; Dore v. Babcock, 74 Conn. 425; Frye v. Bank of 

Illinois, 11 Ill. 367; Phillips v. Kingfield, 19 Me. 375; Boon v. Wethered, 23 

Tex. 675; 1 Greenl. on Ev. 15th Ed. 

461.) "It is what is said of the person under inquiry in the common speech of his neighbors, and members of the community or territory of repute, from which his reputation for truth or falsehood arises, and not what the impeaching witness may have heard others say who numerically may be few and insignificant. "" (Citing Wigmore on Evidence, Section 692.) "It is said in Wetherbee v. Norris, 103

Mass. 565, that in this state no practice as a rule of law is established but it is within the discretion of the presiding judge to require the preliminary question to ascertain if the witness knows the general reputation for truth and veracity in the community where he lives, of the person to be impeached before asking what that reputation is.

But this practice which elicits the fact whether the witness has any knowl edge, yet does not inquire as to its extent, prevents the admission of incompetent testimony, and it was followed with the approval of this court in Com. v. Rogers, 136 Mass. 159." (See 40 

Cyc. 2633-2635. Fyre v. Bank of Ills., 11 Ill. 367, 379.) "The testimony given by Leviston and Rosenberg who each said that he knew what the defendant's general reputation in the community was, showed the requisite qualifications upon the preliminary inquiry. To what extent the weight of their evidence had been affected by the cross examination was for the jury under appropriate instructions. It cannot be said as matter of law that the

as far as the witness knows the character of one is bad, is not sufficient.100 Evidence of a witness's reputation by one who knows only his reputation "in the flour trade,” 101 or by one who has heard it discussed only in the family of the witness's father-in-law,102 is not sufficient. 

2. One's own witness. By statute the character of one's own witness cannot be attacked.108

I. Drunkenness. Evidence of habits of intoxication proved otherwise than by a court record, though possibly in rare cases admissible in the court's discretion, if not entirely necessary as a material part of the complaint or defence, beyond all question, should not be received to show the kind of person that the witness is.104 When offered merely to affect the witness's credibility, as evidence of a criminal offense, it should be excluded. 

If such evidence bears directly on the issue on trial it is admissible.105 "A witness is not on trial for his character." 106

To admit such evidence to attack credibility would confuse the issue, prolong the trial, be unfair to the witness, and create prejudice. In some cases, however, evidence that a witness was drunk or had been drinking is admissible. If his condition may have been such that it affected his ability accurately to observe or to remember, the fact that he was drunk or had been drinking at the time of the occurrence of the event in issue, may be and probably, if properly offered, must be admitted.107 The testimony in chief was wholly incompetent.' (Citing, Rundell v. LaFleur, 6 Allen 480.) "But the evidence of 

Pritchard that the defendant's reputation 'was poor amongst the flour trade,' and of Herman, who had no other information than what had been gained by inquiry from people 'connected with and all in the flour trade," that the defendant's general reputation for truth and veracity was 'very poor' should have been excluded for reasons sufficiently stated. The general verdict in each case was adverse to the defendant, and as this incompetent evidence was prejudicial we have no means of knowing that it did not influence the jury to his harm."' F. W. Stock & 

Sons v. Dellapenna, 217 Mass. 503.

Failure to remember.

J. 

A witness's repeated statement that he does not remember important material details may be considered and employed by the jury, to impeach him or to show him an unreliable witness.110 Failure of memory, however, is not always to be taken as operating against a witness's credibility. A witness's testimony that he does not remember an event, is not the equivalent of testimony that the event did not happen; when a witness states that he does not remember an event or conversation, his prior statement which sets forth the details of the event or the conversation, it seems, can not impeach him.112 If such evidence is erroneously admitted on the assumption that failure of memory creates a right to admit such statement, by a proper charge the error may be corrected.118 

108 The defendant offered to show that the men on the seat of the truck stopped the engine and passed a flask between them which could have been found to contain intoxicating liquor. 

This evidence with other evidence, was admissible as having a tendency to show that Seymour and Swan were intoxicated when the accident happened. 

If the jury so found, it could also have been found that it affected the credibility and weight to be given to their testimony. The exception to the exclusion of the evidence must be sustained. "One Redfern, a motorman, testified that on the day of the accident, and before it occurred, he was operating his car in the same direction as the truck was travelling and caught up with it just before he reached the Winchester line;" the defendant offered to show by this witness for the purpose of proving that the men on the truck were under the influence of liquor that they "were boisterous, noisy, that they drove their auto in front of the car and held it back several times so that because of it the car lost 12 minutes in going from 

Medford to Woburn; that at times when the auto left the track to let the car go by one of the men, the plaintiff's intestate-well, that is a matter of inference but one of the men thumbed his nose at the motorman and 'Ha! Ha! 'd to him." This evidence was competent to show that Seymour and Swan were under the influence of liquor and to contradict their testimony that they were sober at the time of the accident. K. Conviction of crime-1. Adversary's witness-a. Stat utes. To impeach the adversary's witness statute allows evidence of the witness's conviction of crimes and offenses if seasonably offered 114 subject to the following restrictions: "First, the record of his conviction of a misdemeanor shall not be shown for such purpose after five years from the date on which sentence on said conviction was imposed unless he has subsequently been convicted of a crime within five years of the time of his testifying. Second, the record of his conviction of a felony upon which a fine only was imposed, or a sentence to a reformatory prison, jail or house of correction, shall not be shown for such purpose after ten years from the date on which sentence on said conviction was imposed unless he has subsequently been convicted of a crime within ten years of the time of his testifying. Third, the record of his conviction of a felony upon which a state prison sentence was imposed shall not be shown for such purpose after ten years from the date of expiration of the minimum 120 term of imprisonment imposed by the court, unless he has subsequently been convicted of a crime within ten years of the time of his testifying." 121 b. What is a conviction-(1) Generally. A conviction is a judgment and sentence,-nothing less.128 A plea or verdict of guilty,124 a plea or a verdict with a release on probation,125 or a who testified merely that he was unable to recall a certain conversation, by introducing evidence under R. L. c. 175, par. 24, that the witness had had such a conversation. The court charged the jury that if they believed the evidence offered in impeachment then they "just put the testimony of the first witness out of the case. You do not put in the second witness's testimony as affirmative facts" or as having any probative force. Held, that the rights of the plaintiff were protected, and that his exception must be overruled. 

Bloustein v. Shindler, 235 Mass. 440.

A witness without proof of the record of conviction, solely to affect credibility, cannot be proved a prostitute,140 keeper of a house of ill fame,141 or the parent of an illegitimate child; 142 but the mere fact that a witness has admitted orally that he committed a crime, does not render inadmissible all evidence material to the record of his conviction,148 and evidence of one's drunkenness at a time when he observed certain material acts is admissible without a record of conviction of drunkenness to show his lack of power of observation and memory.' 144 (2) Matters to be distinguished. Here two distinctions are to be pointed out. First, that when one is charged under a statute which makes a second offense a more serious and separate crime, evidence of the first offense may be proved in the defendant's cross examination as a part of the charge without production of the record; 145 second, that a plea of guilty in the same or in another case, to establish facts which constituted a part of the offense to which the witness pleaded, and now relevant and material, is admissible.146 Such evidence is not admissible merely to affect credibility.147

O. Business association: Employees: Joint enterprise. A 

witness's close business association with a party may have a tendency to show that his attitude toward that party is friendly and favorable. To show the witness's tendency to favor such party and thus to attack his credibility, such evidence may be presented.167 So the fact that a party and the witness bear the relationship of employer and employee, may be shown. 

Former employment may affect a witness's prejudice. Hence the fact that the witness was in the employ of a party at the time when the event now in issue occurred has been held admissible.168 The amount of compensation of a former employee may be excluded.169 The admission of evidence of this kind, testimony without corroboration; mere failure to give such an instruction is not reversible error. CREDIBILITY—WHO MAY BE BELIEVED 

1409 though it may tend to establish facts which might create undue prejudice, seems to a certain extent a matter of right.170 Parties who are engaged in a joint enterprise are usually in such a friendly relationship that the adversary may inquire as to their status. Service on the same board of directors does not necessarily establish a friendliness.171 

P. Discharge: Request to resign. Employment does not always create friendliness. The true condition may be presented. 

The fact that a party has incurred the hostility of the witness by discharging 172 him, or by requesting him to resign, may be shown by that party when the adversary calls the employee as a witness, to establish his animosity.178 If the evidence of discharge is simply to show animosity of one witness toward another not a party, it well might be excluded.174

Q. Employment by persons other than parties, or persons in interest: Employees of party's insurer. If the real person in interest employs a witness, this may be shown. Hence though the fact that a party is insured against the tort in suit is not admissible to establish his negligence,175 yet, the fact that an expert or other person is in the employ of the insuring company, may be brought out in cross examination to show his interest and to affect his credibility.176

R. Nationality, race, color, religion. The nationality, race, color, or religion of a witness is not admissible in the ordinary case to establish a prejudice or friendliness, or to affect credibility.180 Such evidence may be admissible for other purposes. 181 

S. Indebtedness to a party. Proof of the indebtedness of a witness to a party may be elicited in cross examination to establish the witness's prejudice.182 So the fact that a suit is pending or has been instituted between the witness and party is admissible on the issue of prejudice.188 

T. Promise of immunity. Evidence that a witness has received a promise of immunity in consideration of his testimony against a criminal defendant, is admissible; its weight is for the admissible. (Citing, Mayhew v. Thayer, 8 Gray 172, 177.) In Stevens v. Stewart- 

Warner Speedometer Corp., 223 Mass. 

33, the question excluded on cross-examination was, "who were you working for?" The answer expected was, "The 

Federal Insurance Co. who insured the car against theft." This should have been admitted as tending to affect the weight to be given to the testimony of the witness upon his direct examination. It is true that the action in that case was brought for the benefit of the insurance company which had indemnified the owner. A defendant, who is insured, can properly call a witness, even if as the paid employee of the insurance company he has prepared the case for trial, and may call on him to give evidence of alleged admissions obtained by him in conversation with the plaintiff, which, if believed by the jury, would exonerate the defendant from all liability. If his employment cannot be shown because the answer would reveal that the defendant had a policy of indemnity insurance, an exception is created to the universal rule, that the pecuniary interest of a witness, or his prejudice or bias, in favor of the party calling him can always be shown, limited only as to the extent of the inquiry by the sound discretion of the trial judge. (Citing, Jennings v. Rooney, 183 Mass. 577, 579.)

Stewart-Warner Speedometer Corp. just cited as in the case at bar, is the question of the possible partiality of the witness to which the judge's ruling denying the request to strike out is limited by the report. Dempsey v.

jury. The court should instruct the jury that they might consider whether such witnesses are "under the strong bias of selfprotection." 184

U. Pretense of not understanding English. The effect of cross examination is weakened if the witness does not talk English. In an examination where a witness who really understands 

English pretends that he does not, the witness, on account of the translation through the interpreter, gains the advantage of longer time for preparation of a false answer. Hence, a witness who now claims that he is unable to talk or understand English, may be impeached by evidence that at a former trial he testified in English and appeared to understand questions put in English.185

V. Concealing facts. If a person, out of court, when asked a fair question which pertains to the present issue, refuses to answer, or answers falsely, this may be admitted to show his interest and bias.188

W. Influence of one witness on another. The influence of one witness on another is admissible to show coercion, undue influence, and like matters, if such matters may be found to have affected the witness's testimony or conduct.187 That a party with wrongful motive has urged a witness to insist on 

184 The presiding judge could hardly have told the jury that these witnesses as matter of law were "under the strong bias of self protection and hope of immunity" and that "this bias should be taken into account by the jury in weighing their testimony." The defendant was entitled to have the jury told that they might consider whether these witnesses were "under the strong bias of self protection" and that they might take that into account in weighing their testimony and that was in effect what the jury were told. The presiding judge instructed the jury in these words: "" 'As to both of the cases, consider carefully whether the various witnesses who have come before you, or any of them, are dominated by prejudice,—prejudice against Harris, prejudice against Task, prejudice against any other person connected with this case. Consider whether they are biased in favor of any witness in this case. 

Are they under any promise of some sort, of future benefit or anything of that sort? Are they under financial obligation to anybody connected with the case? All these things you may take into account in weighing the testimony of the various witnesses who have appeared before you." Commonwealth v.

X. False testimony. A witness's false present testimony, such as an untruthful claim of an alibi, may be taken as evidence to affect his general credibility. The mere fact that a witness testifies in part falsely does not require the jury to disbelieve his whole story.189 

Y. Bias, prejudice, friendliness. Evidence tending to show the existence or nonexistence of bias, within reasonable limits, may be sought in cross examination.190 If evidence of lack of bias is admitted when offered by the proponent, though this is error, yet if the adversary later raises the question of the witness's bias, the error is cured.191 The particulars of the circumstances which lead to the creation of the prejudice, or bias, or enmity, usually are excluded.192 The same rules apply to evidence of friendliness.

ter appearing in cross examination only, it is said cannot be refuted by a witness other than the witness who gave the testimony; immaterial matter not brought out for the first time in cross examination may be so contradicted.195

BB. Effect of evidence. Facts disclosed by evidence admitted solely to impeach a witness, are not thereby proved. 

The evidence is admissible solely on the question of credibility, and not to establish the existence of the facts or statements disclosed by the impeaching evidence.196 However, the objecting party should point out the cause of his objection, and should require the court to instruct the jury as to the limited purposes of the evidence.197

CC. Relationship to the hearsay rule: Statements of agents. 

Statements inconsistent with present testimony are not to be excluded merely because they are hearsay. As they are not evidence of the facts which they set forth, their admission does not concern the hearsay rule. A statement by an agent not within the scope of his authority under that rule is not admissible to establish the facts which it embodies, as an admission against his principle; but if the agent takes the stand and testifies to material facts contrary to his statement made out of court, a witness may be called to testify to the inconsistent statement.200 This is true even though the agent is called by the adversary. 201 DD. Explanation of inconsistency. When a party seeks to introduce evidence of inconsistent statements by his own witness he must give the witness a chance to explain.202 When an adversary's witness has been shown to have made contradictory statements, the extent of explanation is within the court's discretion.203

IV. By what evidence may a witness be corroborated—A. 

Generally. A party usually cannot strengthen his testimony by showing directly the high standing of his witnesses.210 He may show the kind of person that is before the jury as a witness, but there usually he must stop.211 

B. Witness's prior consistent statement. We are dealing now with prior consistent statements of a witness, offered solely to support his present testimony.212 Usually a party cannot introduce a prior consistent statement of his witness solely to show the truth of the witness's present testimony.218 In three classes of cases, however, such statement may be introduced, namely "where the contention is made that the testimony of the witness is given under bias or undue influence arising from some occurrence subsequent to the main event; or is a recent contrivance or invention"; 214 or that the facts described in testimony, previously have been concealed under circumstances which warrant the belief that if they were true the witness would have been likely to reveal them.215 This evidence is admissible to support only an impeached witness. Hence if the adversary disclaims any intent to discredit a witness, and the court properly instructs the jury in regard to the effect of such claim, exclusion of the prior consistent statement is proper. 

Several matters are to be noted. If evidence admissible for purposes other than the impeaching of a witness is offered, and the evidence is of such nature that it tends also to impeach the inquiry at the inquest was whether or not you were to blame for the death of Mr. Hollis?" The question was excluded. "The defendant's purpose in asking this question was to show the witness at the time of the inquest, was under a severe mental strain, and that the contradictions in his evidence were to be attributed to this fact. Something must be left to the wise discretion of the judge in such matters. The appearance and manner of the witness, the nature and importance of the contradictory evidence are not before us, and we cannot say that there was error in excluding the question." Godfrey v. 

Old Colony, 223 Mass. 419. But see conflicting dicta and suggested rule, note 12 above.

220"The defendant, therefore, is forced to go the length of contending that whenever from the cross-examination of a witness it is apparent that the other party intends to attack his testimony, the party calling the witness for the purpose of corroborating the witness's testimony can introduce in evidence previous statements made by him which are to the same effect as the testimony given by him on the witness stand. For that proposition no authority has been or can be cited. It is the rule in some jurisdictions that similar statements may be introduced in evidence to corroborate the credibility of a witness which has been attacked by proof of statements made by the witness which contradict the testimony given by him on the witness stand. ness's merely relating a different story at a time previous to testifying does not permit the corroboration of the witness's testimony by a statement still prior to that relation.222 Ordinarily the government to corroborate its witnesses, is "not allowed to put in evidence of what witnesses have said to a police officer, prosecuting officer, or other person; the exceptions are those three that I have mentioned." 223 No decision has come to our notice, and we think there is none, holding that the mere assertion of counsel, such as was made by counsel for appellant in his opening statement to the jury, that 'we expect to show you that it is a frameup,' etc., constituted such an assailing" of testimony as to render evidence "as to previous consistent statements admissible. We also think that the weight of authority is to the effect that the mere assailing of a witness's testimony by cross-examination, though such cross examination may suggest impeachment, does not render it permissible to prove previous consistent statements of the witness in order to sustain or corroborate his testimony given upon the trial."' 224 

To render a consistent prior statement admissible it must have been made before the bias or undue influence existed, or before the "contrivance" or "invention" had operated on the witness's mind, or before the alleged concealing; in other words the prior consistent statement must have been made before the wrongful influence arose.225 The prior consistent statement may have been under oath and on the witness stand.226 In offering evidence of a prior consistent statement the offer of proof should disclose all the elements which make it admissible.287 This subject has been summarized substantially as follows. "The mere fact that a witness has made statements on other occasions at variance with testimony given in court does not warrant the introducing of confirmatory evidence to the effect that he has given an account of the transaction at still other times in harmony with his sworn testimony. A party may, for the purpose of discrediting an opponent's witness, show that he has given two inconsistent narrations of the same affair, one of which was necessarily untrue. As is pointed out with clearness by Bigelow, Jr., in Commonwealth v. Jenkins, 10 Gray 485, 488, when this is the state of the evidence it by no means relieves

Mass. 86.

524.

the witness of the distrust thus cast upon him, to prove that the story last told was similar to an earlier version given by the witness. The two inconsistent statements still remain. Hence under these circumstances, such corroborating evidence is inadmissible. This is the general rule, but there is an exception where the contention is made that the testimony of a witness is given under a bias or undue influence, arising from some late occurrence subsequent to the main event, is a recent contrivance or that the facts described in testimony, previously have been concealed under conditions which warrant the belief that if they were true the witness would have been likely to have revealed them. In such a situation evidence that the witness at earlier times before the intervention of these pernicious impulses had made statements like those given in court has a legitimate tendency to impugn the existence of these factors as operating causes to produce the testimony and thus to fortify his testimony, and therefore should be admitted. The exception to the general rule is a narrow one and is not to be extended; but, when the contentions of the parties give rise to its application, it is well established. Griffin v. Boston, 188 Mass. 475. 

Brown v. Brown, 208 Mass. 290. See for a full discussion of all the principles, Commonwealth v. Tucker, 189 Mass. 457, 470 to 485. 228 

Three classes of cases are to be distinguished. If the prior consistent statement is part of the evidence material to prove the case or establish the defense it is not excluded solely because it corroborates present testimony; 229 self serving evidence to corroborate a party is not usually received; 280 and there is no distinction between "invention" as used above and a "changed story" inspired by a party's influence.231

D. Corroborating facts, documents and other matters-1. 

General rule. It has been said that "when a witness has testified directly to a fact from the experience of his own senses, the extent to which he may be allowed to testify to circumstances corroborative of the truth of what he has sworn must rest in the discretion of the judge who tries the case." 283 Here, even evidence of reason for remembering an event,284 or a witness's mental state,235 may be admitted; and though the facts to be corroborated are admitted as true, the corroborating testimony may be admitted in evidence.236 Many cases illustrate the rule 287 

2. Exceptions to the rule-a. Self serving conduct of a party. This rule is subject apparently to one exception. If a party seeks to corroborate his testimony by self serving evidence of his own acts, such evidence is not admissible. Hence when the issue is, did the plaintiff loan to the defendant, when the plaintiff has testified to the loan he cannot show solely to corroborate his testimony his bank account to prove that he withdrew money at the time of the alleged loan; 238 had there been question of the plaintiff's ability to loan the sum, such evidence it seems would be admissible.239 Another case worthy of note is Waxler v. Bottcher.240 There the issue was whether the de-

E. Corroboration of own expert by showing his employment by adversary. When an expert is called as a witness by one party, it is a wiser exercise of discretion for the court to exclude a question which asks whether in other cases he has appeared as an expert for the adversary.242

F. Corroboration when character is attacked. When a person's character is attacked by showing his conviction of a crime, and probably when his reputation for truth and veracity is attacked, he may probably of right offset the inferences by proof of good character.248

G. Criminal defendant's good character. In a criminal case the defendant's character is always at issue. Evidence of his good character always is admissible. It probably is admissible to show his credibility.244

H. Cumulative evidence. How far material evidence may be corroborated by testimony of other witnesses lies in the discretion of the court. He may, in the use of reasonable discretion, limit the number of witnesses.245

I. Introductory. In Part II the rules of admission and exclusion are considered. Here more particularly we take up the exclusion of certain kinds of relevant evidence which should not be admitted because its admission will do more harm to the community over a period of years, than its exclusion in the individual case. Public policy in such matters, creates an exception to the rules of admission termed "Privilege." Usually, either expressly or by implication, privilege may be waived.¹ 

In some matters, however, as in private conversations between husband and wife, there can be no waiver. So when the question as to how a voter cast his legal vote is involved the court is slow to interpret any act as a waiver of the voter's privilege to refuse to disclose how he voted. Privilege may be considered as having four possible divisions. (1) Absolute privilege,matters within such division, under no circumstances can be disclosed.* (2) Absolute personal privilege,-matters within such division can under no circumstances be disclosed by certain persons. (3) Personal privilege,-matters which can not be dis- 

1 One who voluntarily takes the stand usually waives personal privilege. See 

Gossman v. Rosenberg, 237 Mass. 122. 

See also No. 467 following. See 106 following. 

2 See Absolute Privilege following, numbers 8 to 16, and Absolute Personal 

Privilege, numbers 17 to 89. 

8 See number 10 following, also note 463. 

4 See numbers 8 to 16. 

5 See numbers 17 to 18.

closed without waiver of the privilege by the person whom the privilege seeks to protect, or his representative. And (4) discretionary privilege,-matters of a secret nature which the court will not order a person to disclose unless justice requires such disclosure."

These with their illustrations are now considered. II. Absolute privilege. To-day, it seems, there are few matters within the class of absolute privilege,-few matters which are so sacred that under no circumstances can they be disclosed. 

Even private conversations between husband and wife may be testified to by one who overhears them; the purport of a vote at an election cast under the greatest secrecy, may in certain instances be disclosed by the voter on the stand, or by his verbal admission; 10 the deliberations of a jury usually considered most sacred ¹¹ have been testified to by an outsider who heard their discussion.12 The vote of an individual juror perhaps may be disclosed by direct evidence with the consent of the whole jury, and the court.18 

One matter at least should be absolutely privileged, namely, a religious confession made to a priest or other clergyman for the purpose of spiritual aid or forgiveness. 

By statute, a previous conviction of a witness if it is at a more remote time than that allowed by statute is absolutely barred; credibility cannot be attacked by such conviction. By statute, also, certain reports and returns filed for purposes of taxation, and like purposes are absolutely privileged.14

At common law evidence of a husband or wife which tends to bastardize the issue born during wedlock may fall within this class.15 Statute, however, in certain cases makes this admissible.16

III. Absolute personal privilege-A. Generally. An abso lute personal privilege is one wherein a certain person is forbidden to disclose a certain matter and wherein such privilege as

Two common matters are within this class. 

B. Communications of husband and wife; and other matters which pertain to evidence of husband and wife that might be confused therewith-1. Generally. The relationship of husband and wife is such that public policy requires that they should be permitted to stand to each other in the most secure position of trust and confidence. An absolute personal privilege, as applied to private conversations between them, is created by statute.¹ 

That there may be no confusion, it is well here to point out other matters between husband and wife which fall within the rules of privilege. Between them there are certain matters which fall within the class of personal privilege.18 That the distinction may be made clear this whole subject of privilege between husband and wife is here considered. 

2. Statutes-a. Original statutes creating absolute personal privilege. The absolute privilege between husband and wife is established by statute, and is now embodied in General Laws, Chapter two hundred and thirty-three, section twenty. By the original act, no private conversation between husband and wife could be admitted in evidence.19 

b. Limitations of the original statute: "Conversations": 

"Nonsupport," "neglect," etc., letters: Offer to support. Two important inroads on this original statute have been made. (1) 

Though a private conversation cannot be testified to by either husband or wife, yet abusive language addressed by one to the other is not a conversation, and has been held admissible.⁰ (2) 

By statute 21 in proceedings against a husband for nonsupport or desertion of his wife or minor children, or against a parent who violated certain provisions of that statute, "any existing statute or rule of law prohibiting the disclosure of confidential communications between husband and wife shall not apply.' 28

To show that the trial judge erred in excluding a conversation between husband and wife, the record should disclose that the conversation was not private.42 d. Statutes not to be confused (1) Usual rule. Statutes which bear on "personal privilege" are not to be confused. Here arises the interpretation of a statute which permits a willing husband or wife to testify against his spouse in all matters other than private conversations. The usual rule set by statute in such matters is that "neither husband nor wife shall be compelled to testify in the trial of an indictment, complaint, or other criminal proceeding against the other." 48 (2) Statute creating exceptions. To this usual rule there is a statutory exception set by section seven of Chapter two hundred and seventy-three of the General Laws. That section refers only to proceedings created by that chapter. Section one applies to certain criminal liability for nonsupport, desertion, and neglect of a wife, and of certain minor children. In such proceeding 44 "both husband and wife shall be competent witnesses to testify against each other to any relevant matters, including the fact of their marriage, and the parentage of the child,45 provided that neither shall be compelled to give evidence incriminating himself."' 46

In actions for bastardy and for nonsupport of parents the same rules of evidence with the same modifications apply.47 

(3) Interpretation of these statutes: Right of one spouse to refuse to testify against the other; exercise of privilege: How and by whom. Under this statute, in criminal cases other than those which arise under General Laws chapter two hundred and seventy-three, one spouse may testify against the other, but will not be compelled to do so.48 Here is a personal privilege.49 before marriage. Such conversations are not excluded from being offered as evidence in court. Commonwealth v.

The witness, and not the spouse of the witness, is the one who enjoys, and must elect as to exercising the privilege.50 The witness may waive it or not as he sees fit.51 In a criminal case, if a husband is on trial, and his wife is shown to be in possession of material knowledge as to his guilt or innocence if he does not call her as a witness, this fact may be taken as evidence that she if called would not disclose facts favorable to him.52 He cannot argue that he did not call her because she would have claimed this privilege; 53 nor can he exercise the privilege.54 

Hers is the privilege.55 The proper procedure is for him to call her to the stand and for her, if she desires not to testify, then to claim her privilege under the statute, not to testify.56 If she does so, her failure to testify shall not be considered as evidence against him." In proceedings under General Laws, Chapter two hundred and seventy-three, section one by section 7 either is made a "competent" witness but this privilege is not expressly removed. It is seldom, if ever, that a case under that chapter and section wherein a witness seeks this privilege will be tried. 

It is again to be pointed out that the statutory limitations which apply to proceedings for nonsupport, desertion, and neglect of wife and family, apply to complaints for bastardy and for nonsupport of parents.58 "Practice" as used in sections sixteen and twenty-two of chapter two hundred and seventythree of the General Laws, includes rules of evidence.59

an absolute privilege, is that which excludes evidence by a husband or wife to the effect that during lawful wedlock wherein a child was born they had no intercourse.61 A like principle excludes declarations by an alleged husband and father that he was impotent. The rule has been stated in substantially the following words: Where the legitimacy of a child born in lawful wedlock is in issue, in the absence of statutory authority neither the husband nor the wife may testify as to non-access between them. The rule has been based on reasons of decency and policy, especially because of the effect it may have upon the child, who is in no fault. The rule has been severely criticized."2 

But it has been settled in this Commonwealth.68 Declarations made several years prior to marriage relative to powers of procreation are likewise inadmissible. A husband is not permitted to bastardize a child born in wedlock by testifying that he was impotent at the time of its conception. This privilege is based on public policy as it affects the child and his inheritance.65 The privilege probably cannot be waived by the husband and wife.66 

Possibly the doctrine applies only when questions of inheritance are involved.67

There is to this doctrine against "bastardizing the issue" a statutory limitation. This limitation is set forth in General 

Laws, Chapter 273, section 7, and applies to proceedings under section one and sections eleven to twenty-two, of that chapter.68

C. Jury and jurors: Judges: Grand jurors. The discussions and deliberations of a petit jury are sacred." Jurors usually are not required or permitted to testify as to how they voted or as to what took place in the jury room.72 A third person who overheard their discussion, however, has been permitted to testify that the jury agreed to render a verdict in accordance with the outcome of the tossing of a coin, or of casting of lot." Possibly if all the jurors and the court consent, a juror may testify as to discussions and votes in the jury room,' 74 or jurors may testify to rebut evidence of their misconduct." Undoubtedly a judge is not required to testify as to his reason for his finding or ruling.76

A grand jury stands somewhat differently. As to how its members voted," or their opinions expressed in discussing the evidence,78 or as to the fact that an indictment for felony has been found against an accused not yet apprehended," or as to 

105 Mass. 287, 290. Koffman v. Koffman, 193 Mass. 593.) But St. 1913 c.

7, of the latter provides that "both husband and wife shall be competent witnesses to testify against each other in any and all relevant matters including the fact of the marriage and the parentage of the child or children." "Practice" as thus used is broad enough to include special rules as to evidence. Therefore the statute permits a mother, although married, to testify in a proceeding under St. 1913, c. 593, to which the husband is not a party that some other man is the father of her child. IV. Personal privilege-A. Introductory. A personal privi lege in the law of evidence, is a right peculiar to persons or officers of designated classes, to have excluded certain kinds of evidence. The logical basis for the rule is that by the exclusion, the community as a whole will receive more benefit than by the admission; though in the individual case important material evidence is excluded, yet should the law admit such evidence that

Illustrations of this class of evidence follow. 

B. Husband and wife. The law of personal privilege as applied between husband and wife is considered above." Briefly the rule is that neither husband nor wife in a criminal case shall be compelled to testify one against the other,91 that the privilege is the privilege of the party called as a witness, not of the spouse on trial,92 that an inference may be drawn in proper cases against a defendant who does not call his wife as a witness,98 and that if a spouse called as a witness then claims the privilege no inference shall be drawn therefrom. This privilege is to be distinguished from the privilege which excludes private conversations between a husband and wife.95 

The exceptions to these principles have been pointed out heretofore.96

C. Attorney and client-1. Introductory: "Advice," mat ters included. A client's private communication to his attorney in seeking advice falls within personal privilege.97 The privilege is the client's and not the attorney's.98 Frequently it is said that this privilege extends only to "advice." 99 Advice as here used should be broadly interpreted. A defendant, regardless of his guilt, may retain, and consult with, counsel, and disclose facts and circumstances including the fact that he is suspected of crime; such matters are privileged.100 The privilege extends to all lawful private 101 communications by the client to the attorney who acts as such, which are or seem to the client material to the discussion of the client's rights, liabilities, guilt or innocence. To come within the privilege, there need be no suit 102

4. Communication must be to lawyer. Communications to lawyers only are within this privilege. If the advice is asked from one whom the witness wrongfully believed to be a lawyer, the communication is not privileged.118 However, evidence of interpreters who are necessary in the proper disclosing of privileged matters to a lawyer is within the privilege.114 This principle has been said to apply to the report of an accident made by a party's employee to the party's lawyer in the preparation of the party's case.115 Such decisions are to be questioned. A detective's report does not come within the privilege,116 and a communication to clerks assisting an attorney at law may not be privileged.117 One who consults a district attorney in regard to crimes which he is to prosecute enjoys a limited privilege.118 

If a lawyer is involved in, and advising a client in executing an unlawful conspiracy,119 or if he acts for both parties to the present conspiracy,120 or if he acts under such circumstances that the facts of themselves show a present or future waiver of the privilege,121 or if he acts in certain matters which involve the validity of a will,122 the privilege does not apply. The lawyer to cause the statement to be privileged need not be under retainer.128

The advice must be asked of the lawyer as a lawyer, not merely as a friend.124

c. Other cases involving estates of a deceased. If there is a controversy after the client's death between his estate and those claiming adversely to it, the privilege may be raised by his executors,140 administrators,141 or, in certain cases, heirs; 142 but where "the controversy is not between an estate and persons claiming against it, but is to determine who shall take by succession the property of a deceased person, and both parties claim under him, the reason for the privilege does not exist, and neither can set up a claim of privilege against the other." 148 So in a petition for the revocation of a decree of adoption. When the person who adopted the child is deceased, and the contest is between the adopted child and her lawful heirs, her statements to her attorney, which show that the adoption was forced on her by her husband, the adopted child's father, is apparently admissible 144 d. Illegal transactions. If an attorney participates as a party with his client in an illegal transaction, transactions between the attorney and client in those matters, are not privileged.145 e. Where attorney represents both parties. If the attorney represents both parties to a controversy communications to him are not privileged at the instance of one party.146

Papers delivered to an attorney by his client as a part of his defense to an action cannot be forced by simple request from the attorney's possession against the client's will.150 They are however within the client's control and he can be compelled, if no rule of law forbids, to produce them. Statements made by the client which tend to show the deed that the attorney is employed to draw, a valid deed, may not be privileged.¹ 151 10. Attorney as witness to matters which are not privileged. 

An attorney, even while trying the cases of his client, may be called by the adversary as a witness to testify to matters that are not privileged.152 The mere fact that he learned the facts as counsel does not bring them within the privilege.158 By calling him to testify to such matters the client does not waive his privilege as to other matters.154 It is said that the "identity of the client,155 the authority of the attorney, or the name of the real party in interest in the litigation can never be confidential. But the nature of the party's title or interest, and the motive of the suit, may be confidential.” 166
