D. Offer of compromise-1. General rule. The law favors settlement of controversies; 158 it approves adjustment of cases without trial.159 Hence it allows parties to a dispute to offer to compromise or to pay by installments without admitting the offer to be put in evidence to establish liability.100 "An offer of compromise has long been excluded as evidence of liability." 161 "If two parties are having a lawsuit and they come together by themselves or through their representatives to settle the case and either makes an offer of settlement, the law will not permit such an offer of settlement to be put in evidence as evidence of liability either on one side or another; because the law looks with favor upon the settlement of controversies." 162 

A party may attempt to buy his peace and his offer of such attempt is privileged.168 It is to be noted that if a liability is not disputed,-if there is no "controversy,"-this principle has no bearing.164 

2. Statements to third persons. An offer to buy one's peace, made to a third person, is not an admission of fault and is within this privilege. Hence in an action for deceit arising from the sale of land, a defendant's statement to a witness who had formerly acted as agent 165 in the negotiations, "If there will be no bother I will give one thousand dollars to that woman," is privileged.166 This is not an expression of willingness to pay a just

3. Limitations of the rule-a. Admissions of collateral facts during negotiations. Usually admissions of collateral facts made during negotiations for settlement, are admissible against the party who made them, to establish the truth of the facts stated.188 

In the leading case in Massachusetts, trover of a colt was the issue. The parties met to attempt to compromise and made certain statements as to material facts. At the trial evidence of ent meaning can be inferred. Such an offer is not admissible as evidence which can be used on the question of liability. (Citing, Grebenstein v. Stone & Webster Eng. Corp., 205 Mass. 431, 438, 91 N. E. 411, and cases cited. 

Garber v. Levine, 250 Mass. 485, 490, 146 N. E. 21, and cases cited.) The instruction given was correct in its ruling on an offer of settlement and an offer to buy peace. It was erroneous and prejudicial in permitting the jury to give the statement any other effect than as an offer of one or the other kind. (Citing Davis v. Charles River 

Branch R. Co., 11 Cush. 506, 509.)"

N. E. Ry. Co., 264 Mass. 128; 162 N.

E. 324. Breach of a contract in writing for the employment of the plaintiff by the defendant as a designer; plaintiff, while absent from the defendant's place of business with his consent was unjustifiably discharged by a letter; before he received the letter he returned and had a conversation with the defendant in which the defendant in effect said to the plaintiff that he, the defendant, could get along without a designer for the rest of the season, and "I will give you $300 and you are a good designer, you could get another position"; held, that it could not be said to have been an error for the presiding judge to admit in evidence so much of that conversation. But it was held prejudicial error for the judge to admit later conversations between the plaintiff and the defendant after the plaintiff had received his letter of dismissal in which the defendant urged upon the plaintiff terms of settlement, offers of compromise not being admissible as ad missions of liability. Straus v. Skur nick, 227 Mass. 172.

these statements was offered. The court says as to this evidence: "So far, therefore, as relates to the evidence offered of admissions of particular facts by the defendant, as that he had sold the property in controversy, and that a demand had been made on him for the same, these are clearly admissions and declarations not protected by the rule excluding offers by way of compromise. In connection with this evidence there was admitted a portion of the conversation between the parties, of a more doubtful character, in which the plaintiff said, 'I suppose you would do better by me than you offered yesterday.' If these words were introduced as evidence disconnected with the other facts stated, they would be properly excluded; certainly, if it was proposed to have the jury infer from them that the defendant had made to the plaintiff an offer to adjust their controversy, by payment to him of money, or other valuable consideration. 

But, although irrelevant, we do not think they were, under the circumstances, so far objectionable as to require the verdict to be set aside for this cause. They were introduced in connection with other words proper to be admitted as evidence. So also, when closely examined, they will not be found to contain any admission that the defendant had ever offered to pay the plaintiff anything. They would be entirely consistent with a mere offer by the defendant to adjust the matter upon the plaintiff's withdrawing his suit. They are, perhaps, also, to be taken as the words of the plaintiff, and without being assented to by the defendant. We do not deem them of such a character, or so material, as to justify or require us to order a new trial.” 169 

The mere fact that a third person is present during the negotiations does not affect the privilege. b. Agreements that conversation be privileged. C. Offer to pay damages: To take care of injured person: To pay doctor's bills. When a person offers to repair the damage done by a tort, the line which divides the privileged from the not privileged is difficult to draw.174 Admissibility depends partly on whether there is any controversy at the time when the offer is made, and partly on just what the offer is.175 If there is no controversy there is no privilege.176 If a person sued says "I owe you 100 but will give you 50 to settle," this should not be privileged. So if a man says to one on whom he has inflicted personal injury, "I am at fault and will pay your doctor's bills," this should be admitted.177 On the other hand a defendant's mere request that a plaintiff accept the amount of his claim in installments is privileged; 178 and if an employer calls on an em-

E. Repairs. It is to the advantage of the public that dangerous and defective premises be repaired. Hence one obliged to repair property and to remove from it causes of an accident or injury which resulted from the defect repaired, is permitted to do so without creating against himself admissible evidence to offer of compromise seeking delay and change of terms of obligation of a note citing Straus v. Skurnick, 227 

Mass. 173. 

179 Bernasconi v. Bassi, 261 Mass. 26, the court says: "Wilson v. Daniels, 250 Mass. 359, 364; 145 N. E. 469, 471, is distinguishable in its facts from the case at bar. In that case the plaintiff's wife testified that the defendant after returning from the hospital where he saw the plaintiff told her that 'he would take care of him (the plaintiff) and look after him.' This evidence was held to be incompetent. Merely to take an injured person to a hospital promising to pay for his treatment there cannot be found to be an admission of liability. (Citing Sias v. 

Consolidated Gas Co., 73 Vt. 35, 40, 41; 

Binewicz v. Haglin, 103 Minn. 297, 115 

N. W. 271, 15 L. R. A. (N. 8.) 1096, 14 

Ann. Cas. 225.) In the present case the evidence was that the defendant would 'fix it up, everything.' This statement could have been found by the jury to have been a promise on the part of the defendant to pay, not only the plaintiff's hospital bills, but for all damages sustained by the plaintiff as a result of his injuries, and hence was evidence of an admission of liability.' "As no prejudicial error appears in the conduct of the trial, the entry must be: Exceptions overruled." Bernasconi v. Bassi, 261 Mass. 26.

prove his negligence in maintaining the dangerous premises. "The general rule, based on public policy, is that evidence of the repair of a defect which caused an injury, made after the accident is not admissible to show him who repaired at fault." 182 "Such evidence is not admissible to show negligence or alone to prove the plaintiff's entire case." 188

In some instances, however, evidence of repairs is admissible. 

Evidence that a person repaired premises in certain instances 184 may be admitted to show that the duty to repair was on him,185 or to assist in interpreting an ambiguous lease.186 Locations of defects may be established by conversation had by a party against whom the evidence is offered during repairs; the repairs and the place of repairs, then explain the conversation.187 Evidence of repairs also may be presented to show that the defect could have been removed, that alteration was feasible,188 and in some cases to show the condition of premises before the accident.189 So, that a motorman pushed down a sand plunger after a passenger had been caused to fall by it, is admissible to show the position of the plunger at the time of the accident.190 Likewise, evidence of a party may be rebutted by evidence of repairs.191 The cases on this subject have been collected heretofore. 198

G. Discharge or reprimand of servant as evidence of negligence: Suspense of license. That a servant alleged to have been guilty of negligence which caused an accident was thereafter discharged by the defendant, is not admissible to show that the servant negligently caused the injury.202 That an employer deducted from an employee's wages certain sums on account of his wrongful act, it is said, may not be admissible to prove the servant's negligence; in the case frequently cited as an authority for such principle, the decision depends on the authority of the officer who caused the deduction to act for the employer.208 

In an action for damages caused by negligence in operating an automobile, evidence that the plaintiff's license to operate a motor vehicle was suspended under General Laws, chapter 90, section 22, after the accident, is not admissible; mere suspension of the license has no rational tendency to show the plaintiff's negligence.204

H. Evidence illegally obtained-1. Generally. It seems strange that the Commonwealth which counts among its historically great, James Otis, and which memorializes his opposition to Writs of Assistance, in the admission of evidence, should acquiesce in the government's illegal seizures. This is the more strange when it is observed that the courts of the United States, into the Constitution of which were written at the instigation of the people of that Commonwealth, the Fourth and Fifth Amendments of its Constitution, exclude such evidence. 

However, that may be, a sharp and clear conflict between these courts in the law of evidence on such subjects, exists. 

Provisions of the Constitution of the United States and of the Commonwealth of Massachusetts, forbid certain seizures 

201 Wrongfully admitted, Braun v. Bell, 247 Mass. 437. 

202 Hewitt v. Taunton St. R. R. Co., 167 Mass. 483; Grinnell v. Western 

Union, 113 Mass. 299. 

208 Grinnell v. Western Union, 113 

Mass. 299, at 307, cites Robinson v. 

Fitchburg & Worcester, 7 Gray 92. 

There the statement of a president of a corporation that he thought that the corporation would probably give the plaintiff something, as an opinion it implied a want of authority to bind the corporation. See Coe v. Ware, Jan. 10, 1930, Mass. A. S. 1301, 171 N. E. 732. 

204 Peskin v. Buckley, Nov. 26, 1929, Mass., A. S. 2141, 168 N, E. 791.

without a warrant. The Federal Constitution provides by the 

Fourth Amendment that "The right of the people to be secure in their persons, houses, papers and effects against unreasonable searches and seizures, shall not be violated, and no warrants shall issue, but upon probable cause, supported by oath or affirmation, and particularly describing the place to be searched, and the person or thing to be seized." The Bill of Rights of the Con stitution of Massachusetts provides: "Article 14. Every subject has a right to be secure from all unreasonable searches, and seizures, of his person, his houses, his papers, and all his possessions. 

All warrants, therefore, are contrary to this right, if the cause or foundation of them be not previously supported by oath or affirmation, and if the order in the warrant to a civil officer, to make search in suspected places, or to arrest one or more suspected persons, or to seize their property, be not accompanied with a special designation of the persons or objects of search, arrest, or seizure: and no warrant ought to issue but in cases, and with the formalities prescribed by the laws." Here logically arises the question, if property is illegally seized, may it be presented in evidence? May evidence illegally obtained be considered by courts? If seizure is made on illegal warrants or without warrants, must it be returned on demand and placed back where the defendant need not produce it as evidence against himself? Does it matter whether it is unlawfully seized by an officer of the state or by a mere citizen or other individual? or 

2. The Massachusetts rule. The Massachusetts Rule is clear. 

Evidence illegally obtained, whether by the government,805 by an individual, even if taken forcibly from the person or from a dwelling house, and even if obtained in violation of the Statutes and Constitution which forbids its seizure without a warrant, may be received in evidence.206 "It is proper for a judge of the Superior Court to deny a petition by a defendant, charged with the exposing and keeping for sale of intoxicating liquor with intent unlawfully to sell it, that 'two pint bottles said 

205 Commonwealth v. Donnelly, 246 

Mass. 507; Commonwealth v. Wilkins, 243 Mass. 356. 

206 Commonwealth v. Mercier, 257 

Mass. 353; Commonwealth v. Kozleowsky, 243 Mass. 538; Commonwealth v. 

Porter, 237 Mass. 2; Commonwealth v. 

Tibbetts, 157 Mass. 519, at 521; Commonwealth v. Donnelly, 246 Mass, 507;

Commonwealth v. Courtney, 243 Mass. 

363; Commonwealth v. Wilkins, 243

Mass. 356. 92 C. L. J. (Apr. 1, 1921) 

224; American Bar Journal (May, 1921) 

P. 218. 10 Amer. Bar Journal 889. 

Right to recover property illegally taken by an officer, Commonwealth v. Donnelly, 246 Mass. 507,

to contain intoxicating liquors but the true contents of which are to this defendant unknown' which were unlawfully seized from the person of the defendant without warrant or authority, be returned to him and that the officers of the government be prevented from using them or samples of their contents as evidence in the trial of the complaint against the defendant." 207 "Evidence of the character above described is competent and admissible at the trial of the complaint, notwithstanding the fact that it was procured without color of authority by an unlawful and unwarranted act on the part of an officer which would make him personally liable to the defendant in a civil proceeding." 208 "It is proper for a judge of the Superior Court to deny a petition, presented by one complained against for conducting a liquor nuisance and for exposing and keeping intoxicating liquor for sale with intent unlawfully to sell it, that certain intoxicating liquors seized on his premises be adjudged to have been seized illegally, because police officers unreasonably had exceeded the authority conferred by a search warrant authorizing their seizure, and had destroyed and seized property not described in the warrant;" it is proper for him to deny a request "that the officers and the government's attorney be deprived of the use of such evidence so obtained and that it be returned to the petitioner." 209 "At the trial of a complaint charging the defendant with conducting a liquor nuisance and with exposing and keeping intoxicating liquor for sale with intent unlawfully to sell it, it is proper to admit in evidence intoxicating liquors seized by police officers on the defendant's premises under the authority of a search warrant although in making such search and seizure the officers exceeded their authority in unreasonably destroying and seizing other property not described in the warrant; and to exclude testimony of such acts in excess of authority conferred by the warrant, offered for the purpose of proving that the foregoing evidence was inadmissible." 210 "The intoxicating liquors would have been admissible at the trial above described, even if they had been seized by the police officers without warrant or color of authority." 11 By Massachusetts law illegal search and seizure does not exclude the evidence illegally obtained thereby. 3. The federal rule-a. Generally. In the Federal Courts evidence illegally obtained by a federal officer is not admissible.218 Evidence illegally obtained by one not a federal officer and not in collusion with a Federal officer is admissible.219 The jurisdiction of the Federal Courts under the Eighteenth Amendment of the Federal Constitution, has rendered this principle of active importance, but the same rules, except where special statute exists, apply to all illegal seizures. b. 264 Stanley Amendment to the Na tional Prohibition Act shows the intent of Congress to distinguish necessity of a search warrant in the searching of private dwellings from necessity of search warrant in searching vehicles travelling on the public roads. History of that statute:-The Senate had adopted an amendment that officer's search of "the property or premises of any person" without a warrant would constitute misdemeanor. The House offered a substitute applying only to the search of "private dwellings.” The compromise embodied in Section 6 of the Act, provides punishment of an officer who searches a private dwelling without a warrant or who searches, any "other building or property" without a warrant "maliciously and without probable cause. The Chief Justice said: 

other words, it left the way open for searching an automobile, or vehicle of transportation without a warrant, if the search was not malicious or without probable cause. "The intent of Congress to make a distinction between the necessity for a search warrant in the searching of private dwellings and in that of automobiles and other road vehicles in the enforcement of the Prohi- 

"" 66 "In

able." 265 Of course it cannot be searched on mere suspicion.266 "The measure of legality of such a seizure is, therefore, that the seizing officer shall have reasonable or probable cause for believing that the automobile which he stops and seizes has contraband liquor therein which is being illegally transported. 

If the search is not unreasonable the evidence procured is not privileged. 1267 In liquor cases statute regulates to some extent such seizure, but the distinction is recognized as well in other branches of the law. The guaranty of "freedom from unreasonable searches and seizures by the Fourth Amendment has been construed, practically since the beginning of the Government, as recognizing a necessary difference between a search of a store, dwelling house or other structure in respect of which a proper official warrant readily may be obtained, and a search of a ship, motor boat, wagon or automobile for contraband goods, where it is not practicable to secure a warrant because the vehicle can be quickly moved out of the locality or jurisdiction in which the warrant must be sought." 288 Seizures without a warrant in such case if the search is not unreasonable, will not be excluded as evidence.

As stated above, of course the seizure must not be on mere suspicion; searches of rapidly moving vehicles or boats when the officer reasonably believes that they contain liquor seem not unreasonable. What constitutes reasonable "suspicion" and "reasonable cause to believe" and "reasonable cause to suspect" has been variously defined.269 The court in the following words bition Act is thus clearly established by the legislative history of the Stan ley Amendment. Is such a distinction consistent with the Fourth Amendment? 

We think that it is. The Fourth 

Amendment does not denounce all searches or seizures, but only such as are unreasonable.'' "On reason and authority the true rule is that if the search and seizure without a warrant are made upon probable cause, that is, upon a belief, reasonably arising out of circumstances known to the seizing officer, that an automobile or other vehicle contains that which by law is subject to seizure and destruction, the search and seizure are valid. The 

Fourth Amendment is to be construed in the light of what was deemed an unreasonable search and seizure when it was adopted, and in a manner which will conserve public interests as well as the interests and rights of individual citizens.''

Carroll v. U. S., 267 U. S.

269 The United States Supreme Court defines it thus:

-"This is to say that

has justified the provisions of the statute referred to above, and has declared the law as applicable to liquor cases and the right to the return of property wrongfully seized. "We here find the line of distinction between legal and illegal seizures of liquor in transport in vehicles. It is certainly a reasonable distinction. 

It gives the owner of an automobile or other vehicle seized under Section 26, in absence of probable cause, a right to have restored to him the automobile, it protects him under the Weeks and Amos cases from use of the liquor as evidence against him, and it subjects the officer making the seizure to damages. On the other hand, in a case showing probable cause, the government and its officials are given the opportunity which they should have, to make the investigation necessary to trace reasonably suspected contraband goods and to seize them." 270 "Such a rule fulfills the guaranty of the Fourth Amendment. In cases where the securing of a warrant is reasonably practicable, it must be used, and when properly supported by affidavit and issued after judicial approval protects the seizing officer against a suit for damages. In cases where seizure is impossible except without warrant, the seizing officer acts unlawfully and at his peril unless he can show the court probable cause." 271 The common law distinction between a misdemeanor and a felony in liquor cases is not always preserved in statutes 272; locations, The the facts and circumstances within their knowledge and of which they had reasonably trustworthy information were sufficient in themselves to warrant a man of reasonable caution in the belief that intoxicating liquor was being transported in the automobile which they stopped and searched."

S. 132, it was argued that the right of seizure should be limited by the common law rule applying to arrests for misdemeanor in the officer's presence and that unless he could by his senses detect that the liquor was being transported there was no misdemeanor in his presence. But the Chief Justice pointed out that on the third conviction the offense becomes a felony, and said that under the rule "contended for by defendants the liquor if carried by one who has been already twice convicted of the same offense may be seized on information other than the senses, while if he has been only once convicted it may not be seized unless the presence of the liquor is detected by the senses as the automobile concealing it rushes by. This is certainly a very unsatisfactory line of difference when the main object of the section is to forfeit and suppress the liquor, the arrest of the individual being only incidental as shown by the lightness of the penalty." 1099, (June 1926). "Evidence-Illegal seizure as ground of exclusion.-The defendant, in view of a police officer, put two bottles on the ground, in what was apparently a public place. The bottles were dark colored and consequently the nature of their contents could not be ascertained on external inspection. The seizure is valid and the evidence is admissible.280 (b) A search resulted from an invalid search warrant which was issued on affidavit that the day before the officer had detected odor of mash near the building to be searched; liquor, by virtue of the warrant, was seized, but the evidence of the seizure was excluded though apparently a crime was committed in the officer's presence.281

(c) A building partly a store and partly a dwelling may fall within the rule applicable to dwellings.282 Hence when an officer without warrant enters a garage which is part of and underneath a dwelling house, if no arrest is made, his seizure of liquor therein is unlawful.288 (d) A defendant was arrested on a charge of using the mails to defraud; inspectors who accompanied the arresting officer, after the arrest, without a warrant, searched the defendant's office and took letters and documents; on motion for their return the papers were declared to have been illegally taken and were later excluded as evidence.284 This case should be compared with Weeks v. U. S. There it was held that a defendant charged with using the mails for lottery purposes is entitled to papers taken from his home by officers without a warrant.285 (e) Taking by a federal officer of a paper from the home or office of one accused of crime, when procured purely for evidential purposes is illegal.286 (f) This should be contrasted with cases where such papers are wrongfully taken by one not a federal officer, and not in collusion with the government; then the papers are admissible.287 (g) All of these cases should be contrasted with cases where the evidence is taken as part of lawful arrest, or from the person of the man lawfully arrested, or on the premises from which he is lawfully arrested if used in the commission of the crime for which he is lawfully arrested. "The police have the power and it is also their lawful duty to search the person of one lawfully arrested, and also the room or place in which he is arrested, and also any other place to which they can get lawful access, for articles that may be used in evidence to prove the charge on which he is arrested.288 This is a common law right and duty.289 (h) An officer takes from a prisoner lawfully arrested a trunk check and by it procures the trunk. The evidence procured, in spite of the trespass, if the defendant has not been coerced, is admissible.290

d. What evidence excluded on illegal seizure or procedure.

The Fourth Amendment of the Federal Constitution guarantees the right of the people to be secure in their persons, houses, papers, and effects. The provision was inserted to guarantee to man the privacy of his home and the security of his possessions. Writs of assistance and unlawful seizures had been opposed and despised by the American Colonists from their origin; here was a provision that would forever end all like proceedings.

In cases tried under the Federal Prohibition Act, the interpretation of this constitutional amendment frequently arises. A liquid is seized by an officer; he suspects that it is an intoxicant; later analysis shows that his suspicion is correct. The possessor of the liquor later is arrested. He moves that the evidence be suppressed and the liquor returned. The liquor having been taken illegally must be returned on demand and all evidence which results from the seizure is to be excluded. The analysis results from the seizure. Hence evidence of the amount of alcohol in the liquid as disclosed by the analysis, is to be excluded.291

How far this doctrine will be extended to include matters unlawfully seen or heard by an officer is yet problematical. Can an officer who illegally enters a dwelling house testify that he saw the defendant there commit a crime? If he observed the same offense from the highway would the rule be the same? 

If in unlawfully searching a dwelling house for contraband, he observed the defendant in the commission of a felony, may he leave untouched the contraband, arrest the defendant, and testify against him? Would the answers be varied if he made no arrest? No final answer can be given. Here not only the Fourth

Amendment but also the Fifth, has bearing. The aim of the amendments is to protect the inhabitant in his house, person, papers and effects, to guarantee to him that his house is his castle, and to guard against unfair inquisitions and the "informer" methods of the European and English governments during the fifteenth, sixteenth and seventeenth centuries. Yet the necessity of convicting the guilty has made and doubtless will make inroads on these amendments.292

Certainly one is protected in his papers.298 If papers are il-

If papers are stolen by one not a federal officer, and are delivered by him to a federal officer to be used in the prosecution, their return to their lawful owner will not be ordered.802 The court in such case has said: "We know of no constitutional principle which requires the government to surrender the papers under such circumstances. Had it learned that such incriminatory papers, tending to show a violation of Federal law, were in the hands of a person other than the accused, it having had no part in wrongfully obtaining them, we know of no reason why a subpoena might not issue for the production of the papers as evidence. Such production would require no unreasonable search or seizure, nor would it amount to compelling the accused to testify against himself. The papers having come into the possession of the government without a violation of petitioner's rights by governmental authority, we see no reason why the fact that individuals, unconnected with the government, may have wrongfully taken them, should prevent them from being held for use in prosecuting an offense where the documents are of an incriminatory character.'' 803

The dissenting opinion in that case is of importance. Mr. 

Justice Brandeis, with Mr. Justice Holmes concurring, says in part: The plaintiff's "private papers were stolen. The thief, to further his own ends, delivered them to the law officer of the United States. He, knowing them to have been stolen, retains them for use against the plaintiff. Should the court permit him to do so?" "That the court would restore the papers to the plaintiff if they were still in the thief's possession is not questioned. I cannot believe that action of a public official is necessarily lawful because it does not violate constitutional prohibitions, and because the same result might have been attained by other and proper means. At the foundation of our civil liberty lies the principle which denies the government officials an exceptional position before the law, and which subjects them to the same rules of conduct that are commands to the citizen. And in the development of our liberty insistence upon procedural regularity has been a large factor. Respect for law will not be advanced by resort in its enforcement, to means which shock the common man's sense of decency and fair play." 304

Papers legally seized under a legal search warrant, if material, unless they are seized solely for the sake of procuring evidence 805 are admissible in evidence.306 

Other articles are clearly subject of search and seizure. If they are legally seized they are admissible in evidence; if they are not, they are privileged. Some of these are named in the

805 Gouled v. U. S., 255 U. S. 298.

In this case search warrants were issued to seize "certain letters, papers, documents and writings which relate to and have been used in the commission of a felony." The felony alleged was a conspiracy to defraud the United States through contracts for clothing and equipment for the United States Army. There was no objection to the form of the search warrant but the defendant raised the point that the papers seized thereunder were not in themselves property to which, by virtue of their inherent criminal tendencies or possibilities, the Government was entitled. One of these papers was an unexecuted form of contract with Lavinsky, a stranger to the indictment. 

Not itself being an existing contract injurious to the Government, the defendant contended that it was not subject to seizure simply to furnish evidence against the defendant. "While the contents of this paper are not given," said the Court, "it is impossible to see how the Government could have such an interest in such a paper that under the principles of law stated it would have the right to take it into its possession to prevent injury to the public from its use. The Government could desire its possession only to use it as evidence against the defendant and to search for and seize it for such purpose was unlawful. "The Court applied the same rule to an executed contract with one Steinthall, a stranger, which had also been seized. The Court held that nothing in the evidence showed that the contract, admitted to be executed, was still dangerous to the Government or that its seizure was necessary "in order to prevent further frauds." "The Court held that to seize this contract simply to secure evidence to convict the defendant was unlawful. It will be interesting to attorneys to read the Court's views on the exact limitations on the power of the government to issue search warrants.'' Central Law Jour nal, April 1st, 1921. 

306 Schenk v. U. S., 249 U. S. 47.

following quotation: "The wording of the Fourth Amendment implies that search warrants were in familiar use when the Constitution was adopted and, plainly, that when issued 'upon probable cause, supported by oath or affirmation, and particularly describing the place to be searched and the persons or things to be seized,' searches and seizures made under them, are to be regarded as not unreasonable, and therefore not prohibited by the Amendment. Searches and seizures are as constitutional under the Amendment when made under valid search warrants as they are unconstitutional because unreasonable, when made without them-the permission of the Amendment has the same constitutional warrant as the prohibition has, and the definition of the former restrains the scope of the latter. All of this is abundantly recognized in the opinions of the Boyd and Weeks cases, in which it is pointed out that at the time the Constitution was adopted stolen or forfeited property, or property liable to duties and concealed to avoid payment of them, excisable articles and books required by law to be kept with respect to them, counterfeit coin, burglar's tools and weapons, implements of gambling and many other things of like character' might be searched for in home or office and if found might be seized, under search warrants, lawfully applied for, issued and executed.” 807 e. Trespass ab initio. The doctrine of trespass ab initio does not apply to such an extent that a seizure, lawful when made, shall be unlawful for evidential purposes on account of some later wrongful act of the seizing officer. Hence when liquor is lawfully seized, if the officer who has it later destroys a part of it, this does not render his acts void from the beginning and the evidence resulting from the seizure is not excluded.308 The court in this connection says: "Even if the officers were liable as trespassers ab initio, which we do not decide, we are concerned here not with their liability but with the interest of the Government in securing the benefit of the evidence seized, so far as may be possible without sacrifice of the immunities guaranteed by the Fourth and Fifth Amendments. A criminal prosecution is more than a game in which the Government may be checkmated and the game lost merely because its officers have not played according to rule. The use by prosecuting officers of evidence illegally acquired by others does not necessarily violate the Con stitution nor affect its admissibility. The Fourth and Fifth 

Amendments protect every person from the invasion of his home 

307 Gouled v. U. S., 255 U. S. 298. 

308 McGuire v. U. S., 273 U. S. 95.

by federal officials without a lawful warrant and from incrimination by evidence procured as a result of the invasion. Here there was no such invasion. The seizure of the liquor received in evidence was in fact distinct from the destruction of the rest. 

Its validity so far as the government is concerned should be equally distinct. We can impute to the one the illegality of the other only by resorting to a fiction whose origin, history, and purpose do not justify its application where the right of the government to make use of evidence is involved." 809 f. Seizure by other than federal officers. The seizures provided against by the Fourth Amendment are seizures by officers of the federal government, not by mere citizens or strangers.310 

If a state official or other person acts illegally by express or implied collusion with a federal officer it seems that the seizure is as invalid as if made by a federal officer. g. Requirements and definiteness of search warrant. A search warrant to be valid must be issued on evidence supported by oath or affirmation which disclosed probable cause for the search; 320 the warrant shall describe particularly the place to be searched, and the person or thing to be seized.881 Otherwise the warrant is void.322 In liquor cases no "search warrant shall issue to search any private dwelling occupied as such unless it is used for the unlawful sale of intoxicating liquor, or unless it is in part used for some business purpose such as a store, shop, saloon, restaurant, hotel, or boarding house. The words 'private dwelling' shall be construed to include the room or rooms used and occupied not transiently, but solely as a residence in an apartment house, hotel, or boarding house."' 328 h. How questions raised. Courts during trial are not to be turned from the real issue of a case to consider collateral matters. Hence when evidence is illegally procured, as soon as the illegal seizure is known to the owner, or usually at least before trial, he should either petition or move for the return of the property seized and make demand therefor, request that the evidence be suppressed, and that the search and seizure be declared illegal.325 This should be done before evidence which involves the question of illegal search or seizure is offered. Delay, however, is not always fatal to a defendant's rights. "It would be unreasonable to hold that a defendant was bound to apply for the return of an article which he maintained he never had. Where by uncontroverted facts, it appears that a search and seizure were made in violation of the Fourth Amendment, there is no reason why one whose rights have been so violated and who is sought to be incriminated by evidence so obtained, may not invoke protection of the Fifth Amendment immediately and without any application for the return of the thing seized." "On a trial under an indictment for illicit distilling, all the evidence offered by the government was the testimony of three prohibition agents who went to the defendant's residence in his absence, and seized without a warrant what they believed to be parts of a still. They destroyed all the paraphernalia before trial. The defendant made no objection to the testimony when it was given, defending on the merits. Finally, after the general charge to the jury, he requested that an acquittal be directed, on the ground that the only evidence against him was obtained by unlawful search and seizure. H. 64, 68. Formerly this principle was applied by the Federal courts to evidence obtained by unlawful search and seizure. Adams v. New York, 192 U. S. 

585; Youngblood v. United States, 266

Su- practically abrogated when the preme Court decided that an inquiry must be made when the constitutional question is raised, if it seems probable that there has been an unconstitutional seizure. Gouled v. United States, 255 

U. S. 298; Amos v. United States, 255 

U. S. 313. In these cases, it is to be noted, objection to the testimony was promptly made. It has been held by some courts that failure to object at the time evidence is offered, or to move promptly that it be stricken out, waives the objection. Comm. v. Valsalka, 181 

Pa. St. 17, 37 Atl. 405; State v. Yourex, 30 Wash. 611, 71 Pac. 203. But other courts have been more lenient, viewing the requisite of timely objection simply as a 'rule of practice.' Morton v. 

State, 43 Tex. Cr. Rep. 533, 67 S. W. 

115. See also Reg. v. Gibson, 18 Q. B. 

D. 537. In the principal case, in view of the attitude of the Supreme Court, the leniency extended to the defendant was not unwarrantable. No unfairness resulted to the government by eliminating the testimony at the last minute, for it had no other evidence. That the evidence in this case was testimony and not the seized property itself, does not prevent the defendant from raising the constitutional objection. See Silverthorne Lumber Co. v. United States, 251 U. S. 385, 392."'

Similarity to self incriminating evidence. These questions involved in matters of evidence illegally obtained, are important in matters involving self incriminating evidence. The following subject, in the circumstances, therefore, should be considered.381

I. Self incrimination-1. Introductory. The principle of the common law secured by the constitution that one charged with crime cannot be compelled to testify in any prosecution against himself, and that his failure to avail himself of the statutory privilege of becoming a witness cannot be permitted to create any inference against his innocence is guarded sedulously.840 The Constitutions of the United States and of the 

Commonwealth of Massachusetts provide against self incrimi nation. The Fifth Amendment of the Federal Constitution pro vides that no person "shall be compelled in any criminal case to be a witness against himself." Article 12 of the Massachusetts Bill of Rights provides that no subject shall "be compelled to accuse, or furnish evidence against himself." These provisions were placed in the Constitutions to guard against those practices of earlier days whereby an accused was tormented into confession; but this was not their only aim. Under them clearly a criminal defendant cannot be called against his will to testify against himself. In any case a defendant or witness called as an involuntary witness may claim a privilege against self incrimination. In Massachusetts, as stated above, though evidence is illegally obtained, it is admissible. In the 

Federal Courts a contrary rule applies. The question arises then in the Federal Courts as to whether this provision of the 

Federal Constitution renders inadmissible under the principles set out above evidence which has been procured by a federal officer from an unwilling defendant by process authorized by the United States government; when the United States attempts to compel an inhabitant to produce documents or facts which tend to incriminate him, is he obliged to do so? Clearly in a suit in a court of law or equity he is not required to do so. 

Does the same privilege apply to other proceedings? 

2. Privilege of not testifying. A criminal defendant may refuse to testify; his failure to take the stand shall not be used as evidence against him.341 A slight infringement on this prin-

77 ciple in argument of the State's Attorney may be cured by disclaimer and by instructions.842 If he fails to testify at a former trial, this fact likewise by statute may not be used against him by the Commonwealth in the present trial of the same case.348

846 Minimum wage statute. ""There is no criminal element about the act so far as it concerns the employer. 

The facts which the commission is authorized to ascertain and the evidence which it is empowered to seek from employers cannot form the basis of a criminal proceeding, because no crime is created and no prosecution is provided for. Revealing the information or answering the questions required by statute cannot subject the employer to penalty or forfeiture, and does not expose him to imputation of crime. Therefore the constitutional prohibition against a subject's being "compelled to accuse, or furnish evidence against himself" is not violated. (Citing, Art. 12, of the Declaration of Rights.

Not only the defendant in a criminal case but any witness or party in any case may claim this privilege.354 Though the privilege is the witness's and not a party's, yet a party against whom privileged testimony of a witness is wrongfully admitted, it seems, may have a valid exception. 

C. Time and manner of claiming this privilege. The court may require the witness to claim the privilege personally and not by attorney.858 The court is not obliged to tell the witness of his rights to refuse to testify.357 In bankruptcy, however, it is said that the referee has the duty to warn the bankrupt of his rights.358 The witness in objecting to a question should disclose his grounds for refusing to testify. The privilege should be claimed as soon as any question involving privileged matter that pertains to the specific crime, is asked.359 Testifying as to matters which would have fallen within the privilege because they pertain to one crime, does not waive the right to rely on the privilege in so far as another crime is involved.860 Though the defendant's failure to testify at a previous trial for the same crime as that for which the defendant is now tried, is not admissible in the present trial when offered by the government against him,361 yet if a witness at a previous trial has voluntarily taken the stand and without objection has testified as to self incriminating matters, he has waived his privilege, and must testify thereto in the present trial.368 Here it is to be noted

4. Privilege other than testimony in legal trials-a. Introductory. Whether this privilege includes matters other than oral or written testimony offered in courts or proceedings, on some decisions, seems arguable.870 Clearly the privilege is not confined to testimony at trials.371 A man however, in all places, is not immuned from presenting facts which will show his innocence.872 If he were, a defendant's admission by failure to deny accusations of crime made in his presence before his arrest, would not be admissible against him in a criminal case.878 Evidence which tends to show one's innocence, is not self incriminating. The question arises, may the legislature enact that an accused must present at certain times certain documents, or be guilty of an offense in not doing so.374 If the document is one which discloses a crime committed by the person who is so ordered, and is called for solely to establish his guilt, apparently it may not. c. Documents apart from legal trials (1) Introductory. 

A self incriminating document by legislative act cannot be forced from the defendant solely to be used against him in a criminal prosecution.886 This is subject to apparent modifications. The legislature may compel an inhabitant to display a license under which he acts.887 So, under the former or suspended liquor license law, a licensed dealer might be compelled to display at his place of business his license. Frequently the legislature enacts that a person required to file a report with the government, shall be exempt from criminal prosecution on matters disclosed, or that the report shall not be used in evidence against him.388 Such acts, probably avoid any question of privilege; however, the opinion of Mr. Justice McReynolds quoted below is not to be overlooked.889 (2) Automobile registration and driver's license. 5. Procedure in federal courts: Promise of pardon: Conflict of state and federal courts: Bankruptcy. The Federal 

Courts link this rule which arises under the Fifth Amendment of the Federal Constitution, with the rule under the Fourth Amendment. What is said above therefore, as to the admission of evidence illegally obtained in the Federal Courts, on this principle, has bearing.

The treatment of this privilege by the Federal Court arises most frequently in bankruptcy cases. The Bankruptcy Act requires a bankrupt at the first meeting of his creditors and at such other times as the court shall order, to submit to examination on certain subjects. The act adds "but no testimony given by him shall be offered in evidence against him in any criminal proceeding.” 402

44444 Here at the outset is to be noted a statement by Mr. Justice 

McReynolds in effect that the above section "is not a substitute for the protection of the Fifth Amendment, since it does not prevent the use of his testimony to search out other evidence to be used against him or his property.408 By virtue of this principle, it seems, a bankrupt would not be obliged in the absence of waiver to disclose incriminating facts. Merely appearing for examination under the act does not constitute such waiver; and a disclosure by a witness which does not amount to an actual admission of guilt or of incriminating facts, does not deprive him of his privilege of "stopping short" in his testimony whenever it may fairly tend to incriminate him.404 So filing schedules which do not disclose guilt by an involuntary bankrupt without objection, is not a waiver of the privilege.405 

In such case it is said the privilege of the Amendment applies if it cannot be said that the questions propounded considered in the light of the circumstances disclosed, may be answered with entire impunity." 406 In the Federal Court apparently no enactment of the government which does not entirely protect the witness compelled to give evidence against himself from absolute future prosecution as a result of his testimony, will remove his privilege. Even a promise of pardon for the offense by the president will not take from him his right to refuse to incriminate himself.407

A man apart from a bankrupt, or one affected by an act similar to the Bankruptcy Act, is not obliged to turn over evidence to a Federal officer. The Bankruptcy Act requires that the books and certain other property of a bankrupt shall pass to his trustee in bankruptcy. Hence, regardless of the Fourth and 

Fifth Amendments, the bankrupt is obliged to turn over these books and this property. "One who becomes a bankrupt has no right under the Fourth and Fifth Amendments to resist de U. S. 71 at 73, quoted in text below.

In these very instances, however, the Fifth Amendment cuts across the Act with the privilege against self incrimination." It is the duty of the referee so to warn the bankrupt.416 The rule is the same in the matter of schedules; a bankrupt may omit entirely from his schedules any incriminating items."' 417 "To be sure, the usual rules as to incriminating evidence apply to discourage abuse of the privilege. Bankruptcy-Constitutional Law-Duty of Bankrupt to Surrender Incriminating Documents to Trustee. The district attorney had indicted the bankrupts for an alleged crime arising out of the business. The referee ordered the bankrupts to deliver to the trustee books of account relating to the business. Part of the books were surrendered and were immediately taken by the district attorney under a subpoena duces tecum against the trustee. Court seems to be taking the distinction, concurred in by Wigmore,426 that the privilege protects a person only from disclosure sought by legal process against him as a witness, and not from disclosure incidental to the effect of other process. Analogous reasoning upholds the constitutionality of section 70 itself; the disclosure of evidence is incidental to the main purpose of the transfer of title. In the light of the history and purpose of the privilege, the distinction seems well taken. The question of conflicts and comity here arises. When papers are in the hands of a federal officer, such as a trustee in Bankruptcy, may the State Court require their production for criminal prosecution against the bankrupt? Apparently no, unless the Federal Court consents. "Books and papers in the possession of a receiver in bankruptcy appointed by a federal court cannot be taken by a subpoena issuing from a state court, unless the federal court exercising its discretion with due regard for comity, shall consent." 484 The privilege, however, is with the federal court, not with the bankrupt. 6. Conflicts. As said above the privilege against self incrimination protects against disclosing evidence which will tend to convince the witness of a crime against the state of trial, not of a foreign state.486 Where there is a double jurisdiction and enactment within the same territory, as in liquor cases in which the Federal Government and the state government by concurrent legislation seek to accomplish the same purpose, possibly the privilege of one jurisdiction extends to protect a person from testifying to matters which will tend to incriminate him in the other.487 Cases arising under the bankruptcy law are similar; there if documentary evidence is in the possession of the Federal Court, the consent of that Court must be obtained to compel its production in the state court.488 

7. Effect of exercise of privilege: Inference from refusing to answer at the trial: Inducing others to exercise privilege. 

By statute no adverse inference shall be drawn against a criminal defendant who exercises this privilege not to testify. When a defendant induces a witness to rely on the witness's privilege a different question arises. If one in good faith tells another who is about to testify that he can stand on his constitutional rights, no inference can be drawn against the one making this suggestion; but if there is a corrupt suggestion along that line, not standing alone, but taken in connection with other evidence in the case, the jury has the right to draw such reasonable inference therefrom as it thinks proper.489 Though failure of a criminal defendant to testify at a former trial of the case now, by statute, cannot be introduced by the Commonwealth as evidence of guilt, yet in prosecution for subornation of perjury, permitting examination of a witness as to his attitude in refusing to answer certain questions at a former trial is not prejudicial to defendant.440 If a party objects to evidence absolutely

Usually however, if a party takes advantage of a personal privilege this fact may be considered against him. This subject is treated under various subjects above. 

8. Duty of court. The court determines whether the evidence sought is self incriminating.441 The defendant's mere statement that it is self incriminating is not conclusive.442 

9. Effect of conviction or acquittal. If the witness has been convicted or acquitted of the crime in regard to which he claims the privilege, the privilege apparently does not exist. Having been tried, he cannot again be tried. Hence the reason for the privilege has disappeared. 

10. Similar matters not to be confused. This privilege against self incrimination is not to be confused with the obligation of one to call witnesses to meet a prima facie case against him. The privilege does not extend to relieve even a criminal defendant, from this obligation.444 

441 Phillip v. Chase, 203 Mass. 556, 201 Mass. 444. "Although asserted under oath, the claim of Koukouras that an answer to any one of the interrogatories would tend to incriminate him and is conclusive on that question is not sound. It is for the judge to determine whether an answer to any question may reasonably have a tendency to incriminate the witness'' (Citing 

Bull v. Loveland, 10 Pick. 9, 14; In re

"There was no error in the refusal of the court to require the witness West on cross-examination to answer the question: 'Doesn't make any difference to you that you got the statement from these men after you had told them you wanted the statement to use in an investigation for the Federal courts and that the district attorney of the State courts had nothing to do with it?' The defendants' exception 15 rests upon their contention that the statements to the witness West were privileged statements which the court should not allow to be divulged or received without evidence that the privilege was waived by the United

States of America, to be evidenced by some letter or authority from the At torney General of the United States. 

(Citing, Worthington v. Scribner, 109 

Mass. 487, 12 Am. Rep. 736.) "Indisputably the accepted law in relation to privileged communications to the government is that 'Courts of justice will not compel or allow the discovery of such information, either by the subordinate officer to whom it is given, by the informer himself, or by any other person, without the permission of the government'." (Citing 

Worthington v. Scribner, 109 Mass. 487, 489, 12 Am. Rep. 736, Vogel v. Gruaz, 110 U. S. 311, 4 S. Ct. 12, 28 L. Ed. 

158.)" The principle thus laid down is not primarily for the protection of the witness or of the informer, but is adopted on the ground of public policy and because of its importance to the public. It does not apply when the informer is known or when the communication has been divulged. It is patent that a voluntary statement in the way of a confession of crime, as here, made to the government, does not contemplate governmental protection to the name of the declarant or the exclusion of the communications in any proceedings in any court where the name of the declarant and the subject matter of his communications are material and relevant to the issue in hearing, if the name of the declarant and the subject matter of the communications have ceased to be a secret of the government, and especially if the name of the declarant and the facts disclosed by the communications have become matters of general notoriety." "There is nothing in the contention 'that the statement of Getchell was privileged under the laws of the United States and cannot be used in any proceeding against him of whatever name, nature or description, and the testi mony is in violation of his rights under the Fourteenth Amendment and the 15th Amendment to the Constitution of the United States.' It is assumed that the 'fifth amendment' was intended instead of the 'fifteenth amendment.'"'

The plaintiff in an action for wrongful death secured a subpoena duces tecum, requiring the district attorney to produce at the trial a record of questions asked by him and answers made by the defendant's truck driver in the course of an investigation following the driv er's arrest for the killing of the plaintiff's intestate. The district attorney appealed from the order directing the issuance of the subpoena, on the ground that these statements were privileged.

Held, that public policy demands the protection of those giving information

When parties agree that matters shall be treated as privileged the court shows a present tendency to a limited extent to respect their agreement.461 Hence, if parties to a dispute meet to attempt to settle their disputes, and agree that all statements in the conference shall be privileged the agreement is not entirely void, and if during the conference one party makes to the district attorney in his investigations of crime. V. Discretionary privilege. Certain matters may be privileged within the discretion of the court. Possible instances are evidence of trade secrets,468 the presentation of papers privileged in one jurisdiction, for use in procuring conviction in other jurisdictions,464 and evidence of certain matters which tend to degrade a witness.465 In several jurisdictions evidence of such matters will be submitted, in the absence of a positive rule of law, only when the court considers that justice requires that it permit their use or disclosure.466

Of course absolute privileges and discretionary privileges are not waived thereby. Voluntarily taking the stand doubtless waives the right to insist on the privileges which arise from the relationship of attorney and client, and from self incrimination. It is doubtful whether the privilege which arises to protect one who has repaired defective premises, or has taken insurance against accident, is thus waived. Certainly private conversations between husband and wife cannot be disclosed even in the face of express waiver; voluntarily taking the stand possibly removes the privilege of one spouse as to refusing to testify against the other. The general rule is, that voluntarily taking the stand tends to waive every waivable personal privilege.

VII. Effect of claiming privilege: Inferences. As stated above,468 if a party objects to evidence which is absolutely privileged, or which is within the personal absolute privilege, or which is excluded by some rule of law, no inference against him from such objection is to be drawn. If, however, he exercises a mere personal privilege, this permits the jury to infer that the evidence if admitted would not be favorable to him.4 469

I. Introductory. "The ascertainment of facts having probative force on the issue, nothing more and nothing less, is the whole object of a trial in court."1

Any person competent to testify may be called as a witness and fully and fairly examined in chief; this gives to the adversary the right of cross examination. Cross examination is not confined wholly to the cross examination of witnesses of the adversary. A party may be called as a witness by his adversary and when so called he may be cross examined by his adversary." 

The general direction of the examination lies in the court's discretion." He should not permit the examination to be needlessly lengthened or to wander from the issue." In this regard, attorneys owe a duty to attempt to hold the evidence close to the

"Loyalty to the court, a sense of justice, a due appreciation of their obligation to the public should govern attorneys in all actions in conducting a case, particularly in the examination of witnesses. Trials ought not to be permitted to be too prolonged. Examination of witnesses ought to be as brief as practicable. Questions should be concise and directed to a definite object, not rambling and scattered. The trial ought continuously to march forward without repetitions and without diversion into collateral or disconnected matters. The judge ought to be always the guiding spirit and the controlling mind at a trial in this as in all other particulars." Nevertheless a party may present his whole case, and the court must furnish him a reasonable opportunity to do so.10 The court's discretion, within this principle, determines whether questions once answered may be again asked," how many witnesses may be permitted to corroborate a fact already in evidence, 12 the length of examination,18 and the method of the examiner.¹ª 

II. Direct examination. A party presenting a witness should examine him on matters which establish his case. His questions should be brief, clear, and to the point.15 The proponent, in the court's discretion, may ask the ordinary witness, though called by him, leading questions.16 This is so even though the examination tends to violate the rule that the proponent shall not seek to impeach his own witness.17 A party may call the adverse party as a witness and shall be allowed to cross examine him.18 "The extent of the cross examination rests largely in the discretion of the trial judge," 19 who usually, in such matters, allows an extensive cross examination.20

A frequent objection to a direct examination is that a question is leading. In this regard two matters are particularly to be noted; first that a question which suggests equally the answer "yes" and "no" and containing no suggestion of fact, is not leading; 21 and second, as stated above, the trial court in his discretion may allow a party to ask his own witness leading questions. 

A question should solicit answers within the knowledge of the witness; 22 facts and not opinions are usually sought.28 

III. Cross examination-A. Right to cross examine. "A 

witness, and an adverse party, may always be subject to a strict cross examination, as a test of his accuracy, his understanding, his integrity, his bias, and his means of judging. 99 80 B. Scope of cross examination. "In cross examination an adverse party is usually allowed a great latitude of inquiry, limited only by the sound discretion of the court, with a view to test the memory, the purity of principle, the skill, accuracy and judgment of the witness; the consistency of his answers with each other, and with his present testimony; his employment and habits."' 81 

C. Aim of cross examination. The aim of cross examination is to test the credibility, bias, prejudice, accuracy, intelligence, memory, power of observation, and honesty of a witness, to break down or to weaken his direct testimony, or the testimony of the adversary as far as it affects the adversary's direct case. 

A reasonable cross examination for these purposes is a matter of right. The trial court may refuse to allow a defendant to put in facts which pertain only to his defense, during his cross examination of the adversary's witnesses.38 It is seldom, however, that a cross examination is thus limited. The trial court also may refuse to allow a cross examination to bring out purely collateral matters.84

24. The court interfered with counsel for the defendant in an attempt to have the stenographer read notes. Court said, "This is an awfully tedious examination. That is too much.” Court refused an exception to the refusal to read the stenographer's notes. Com. v. Peakes, 231 Mass. 

449, 232 Mass. 450. Defendant contended that the plaintiff's present physical disability was largely or wholly due to an accident sustained by her six years before her alleged injuries by being pushed from the elevator, and she was asked by the defendant on her cross-examination to describe this previous accident. The plaintiff admitted that she had denied to the defendant's examining physician that she had had a previous accident, and stated in explanation of her denial that she would not call the occurrence an accident, whereupon the defendant's counsel asked her, "How much did you receive in settlement of your claim?" The presiding judge excluded the question.

The defendant stated that the answer would have shown that a substantial sum of money was paid in settlement and that the amount received would shed light on the seriousness of her previous injuries and also upon the veracity of her statement to the examining physician that she did not call the previous occurrence an accident. 

Held, that the extent of the cross-examination was within the discretionary control of the presiding judge both as to the collateral matter of the compromise settlement and as to the veracity of the witness. Chase v. Boston El. Co., 

232 Mass. 133. Cross-examination of the defendant's foreman, who was called as a witness for the defendant, to elicit the reasons why a former employee of the defendant who had testified for the plaintiff, had been discharged, was wholly within the discretion of the trial judge. Del Visco v. Gent Elec.

may refuse to permit a party to bring out in cross examination of his adversary's witness, before he opens his case, facts which pertain wholly to the cross examiner's case.37

The court may allow a wide range in a cross examination which tends to shake a witness's credibility. In this respect he may admit evidence to injure the witness's character and permit inquiry as to the witness's way of life, his associates, his habits, prejudices, physical defects and infirmities, and mental idiosyncrasies, his source of knowledge, power of discernment, memory, and description.38 In a leading case the cross examiner was permitted to examine a defendant on trial for murder, who showed anarchistic tendencies, by inquiring, "Is your love for this country measured by the amount of money you can earn?" "What is the reason you came back from Mexico if you did not love money then?" "Don't you know that each year Harvard

University educates more boys of poor people than any other university in the United States of America?" "Don't you know that each year there are scores of them that Harvard educates free?" "Did you intend to condemn Harvard College?" "Were you ready to say none but the rich could go there without knowing about offered scholarships?" "Do you know how many children the city of Boston is educating in the public schools free?" "Do you subscribe to any papers?" "Was the printing of that paper stopped during the war?" "Were they anarchistic papers?" "Were any of the books that were in your house anarchistic?" "Were you afraid of deportation yourself?" "Were your views with respect to anarchy substantially the same as Fruzetti's?" "And you are a man who tells this jury that the United States of America is a disappointment to you?" and "Why did you tell me a falsehood that on Thursday the day before you read the account in the paper, you worked all day?'' 39

E. Length of cross examination. As stated above the length of cross examination is in the court's reasonable discretion." 60 "It was the right and duty of the judge to restrict the examination within reasonable limits, and no harm was done thereby." 61

G. Party as witness: Cross examination of own witness. A party may be called by his adversary and cross examined, the scope and order of such examination is in the court's discretion.68 When the examination has ended the court may refuse to allow counsel for the party thus called to cross examine him, and may limit the scope of the present examination by the party's counsel.64 The Court may allow cross examination by the party who calls a witness other than an adverse party, and in such examination may permit the cross examiner to contradict the testimony of his own witness; 65 but this privilege should be allowed in unusual cases only.

At the trial against a man and a woman for the murder of the husband of the woman, where there had been evidence tending to show illicit relations between the man defendant and the woman defendant, and the man had testified that he had had such relations but at the urgent solicitations of the woman, and on cross-examination of the man there were put in evidence entries in what was a "sort of diary" kept by him from which it appeared that he had had such illicit relations with the woman, it is not an improper exercise of the judge's discretion to permit him to be asked on further cross-examinations, aren't you man enough to take the blame for your relations with (the women) yourself?" Cross-examinations-One by the defendant as a secretary and bookkeeper subject to exceptions by the defendant was asked as to whether on sundry occasions, knowledge had come to her that "the authorities" were making an inquiry as to the defendant's "selling dope." All answers were in the negative. Without determining whether the exceptions to the evidence should be sustained, this court stated that the method of cross-examination was highly improper and prejudicial to the defendant, being an attempt by unfair means to belittle him and render him unworthy of respect and credit.

In an indictment, robbery was alleged to have been committed in May 1917.

In cross-examination of the defendant at the trial, the district attorney, subject to an exception by the defendant, was permitted to make several inquiries as to whether he filed or caused

I. Cross examination in collateral matters. "It is elementary law that a witness in the discretion of the judge may be cross examined as to collateral facts if they be of such a nature as to affect the credibility of that witness in a matter relevant to the issue on trial. It is also a general rule as to all collateral inquiries, whether relating to character or not, that the answers cannot be contradicted" by a witness other than the witness who is under cross examination." If a party cross examines a witness upon a collateral matter, he must take the answer as it is given; he cannot contradict it, and thus raise an immaterial and foreign issue, of which the other party has no notice, and which he may not be prepared to meet." 7ª to be filed for him a petition in bankruptcy in 1913, to all of which he replied negatively. When the first question was put, the defendant asked if the record was to be offered, and the reply was that the "information will be forthcoming in due time." No record of any bankruptcy proceedings was offered at any time. This court, because another exception of the defendant was sustained, did not deem it es sential to decide whether it was reversible error to admit the evidence, but stated that such a method of crossexamination was highly prejudicial to the defendant, and that, irrespective of whether the district attorney believed that a petition in bankruptcy had been filed, an unfair advantage was taken of the defendant in putting the questions. Com. v. Homer, 235 Mass. 526.

In an action of contract, the plaintiff after an auditor had filed a report, amended his declaration so that he claimed more than that claimed in the original declarations and the action was recommitted to the auditor who filed a supplemental report, it is not a violation of R. L. c. 173, par. 85, for the defendant's counsel, in cross-examination of the plaintiff at a subsequent trial of the action before a jury, while holding the pleadings in his hand to inquire when the plaintiff first had made the claim which was the basis of his action and whether he had not prepared and given to his counsel a statement in which he fixed the amount claimed at a sum considerably smaller than that to which he had testified.

If the direct examination has opened matters collateral to the issue the court's discretion determines whether cross examination as to these matters shall be permitted.78 If, on the other hand, cross examination of an adversary's witness, not a party, for the first time brings out immaterial matter, the cross examiner cannot call on other witnesses solely to contradict this evidence given on cross examination."

J. Number of counsel to cross examine. The court may require that a witness be cross examined by one counsel only."5 

IV. Re-direct and subsequent examinations. A re-direct examination may explain a witness' position taken in cross examination, and may meet matters there opened. It is not, as a matter of right, to take the place of the direct examination, and to set out matters which should have been disclosed in direct examination. "Clearly a party has a right in re-direct examination of a witness to ask an explanation of testimony given only in cross examination or to call his attention thereto as a basis of future evidence relating to the same subject, and may 

78 See Note 69 above. The defendants on separate complaints were convicted of being drunk by the voluntary use of intoxicating liquor, and the defendant Dale was also convicted of operating a motor vehicle while under the influence of intoxicating liquor. (1) 

The mother of the defendant Dale testified without objection, in reply to a question by the district attorney, that her daughter had never taken a drop of liquor in her life. The defendant 

Dale thereafter took the stand and testified in cross-examination that she was not a drinking woman, and then, subject to the defendant's exception, in answer to a question by the district attorney, stated that she had taken a drink now and then. The inquiry was competent to test the accuracy and meaning of the defendant's testimony that she was not a drinking woman. 

The extent to which the defendant, who had testified that she was not under the influence of liquor, might be cross-examined as to her drinking habits rested largely in the discretion of the court. (Citing Commonwealth v. 

Corcoran, 252 Mass. 465, 486, 148 N. 

E. 123; Commonwealth v. Sacco, 255 

Mass. 369, 441, 151 N. E. 839; Commonwealth v. Knight, 257 Mass. 421, 425, 154 N. E. 91.) (2) The evidence was also admissible in the discretion of the judge to contradict the testimony of the defendant's mother, brought out in cross-examination, even if that testimony were immaterial. (Citing Commonwealth v. Russ, 232 Mass. 58, 81, 122 N. E. 176; Commonwealth v. Mercier, 257 Mass. 353, 374, 153 N. E. 834. 

See also Commonwealth v. Wakelin, 230 Mass. 567, 576, 120 N. E. 209.) But the testimony of the defendant's mother was not immaterial. It tended to support the defendant's denial that she was guilty of the offense charged. (Citing Commonwealth v. Goodnow, 154

A witness in re-direct may correct or modify his testimony given in cross examination." "If counsel desires to show that the defendant has made a mistake in his cross examination, his attention should have been directed to it by proper inquiry, and he should have been given an opportunity to correct his previous statement. ""78

If cross examination opens new matter, re-direct may be permitted to meet it. Sometimes this opens to a party evidence which pertains to matters which by some rule of evidence were not to be admitted in direct examination though of probative value. Hence though a witness is not, and could not be qualified as an expert, yet if the cross examiner asks his opinion in questions which an expert only could answer, in re-direct examination the witness may be asked further along the same line.79 

When the re-direct examination seeks to bring out new matter not suggested by the cross examination, how far such examination shall be allowed lies in the court's sound discretion.80

In an action by a workman against his employer for having the fingers of one hand cut off by a planing machine on which he had been set at work by an employee of the defendant whom he had been told to obey, the plaintiff introduced evidence to show that at the time of the accident the machine was operated without a back roll and that it was defective and dangerous because of the absence of the back roll. If counsel has forgotten in direct examination some material matter, usually the court will allow him to introduce that matter in the re-direct examination. However, the evidence admissible in direct examination should not be held back and fully presented in re-direct; and a witness should not, by repetition at the end of his complete examination, be permitted to emphasize his former testimony. The "extent to which re-examination of a witness may be carried with reference to matters not testified to in cross examination is within the discretion of the court.", 81

Whether evidence offered in rebuttal shall be excluded because not offered in the direct, lies in the court's discretion.82

In re-direct, as in direct, leading questions may be permitted.88 

V. Other matters pertaining to examination: Objection: 

Witness's answer before time elapses for objection: Offer of proof. Other matters pertaining to the examination of witnesses have been considered heretofore.84 The form, time, and manner of objection,85 the necessity and form offer of proof,86 the method of impeaching and of corroborating a witness,87 and the general powers of the court,88 attorney,89 and witness are considered elsewhere. 90 It is well to point out that if a witness answers before a party has a chance to object to the question, the proper course for the injured party is to state that fact to the court and to ask that the answer be stricken out.91 The record on exceptions should show that the witness answered before the adversary had time to object, and that the procedure above suggested was substantially followed. judge to exclude it when offered by him in rebuttal.
